remember best acc@1 and save checkpoint\n        is_best = acc1 > best_acc1\n        best_acc1 = max(acc1, best_acc1)\n\n        save_checkpoint({\n            'epoch': epoch + 1,\n            'arch': args.arch,\n            'state_dict': model.state_dict(),\n            'best_acc1': best_acc1,\n            'optimizer': optimizer.state_dict(),\n        }, is_best)\n\n\ntrain_step_ct = 0\ntrain_max_batch = 0\ntrain_ips_list = []\n\n\ndef train(train_loader, model, criterion, optimizer, epoch, args):\n    batch_time = AverageMeter('Time', ':.3f')\n    data_time = AverageMeter('Data', ':.3f')\n    losses = AverageMeter('Loss', ':.3f')\n    top1 = AverageMeter('Acc@1', ':.2f')\n    top5 = AverageMeter('Acc@5', ':.2f')\n    ips = AverageMeter('IPS', ':.1f')\n    progress = ProgressMeter(\n        len(train_loader),\n        [batch_time, data_time, losses, top1, top5, ips],\n        prefix=\"Epoch: [{}]\".format(epoch))\n\n    if args.benchmark == \"exact\":\n        pass\n    elif args.benchmark == \"gact\":\n        gact.set_optimization_level(args.alg)\n        controller = gact.controller.Controller(model)\n        controller.install_hook()\n\n    # switch to train mode\n    model.train()\n    for (i, (images, target)) in enumerate(train_loader):\n        images = images.cuda(args.gpu, non_blocking=False)\n        target = target.cuda(args.gpu, non_blocking=False)\n        \n        start = torch.cuda.Event(enable_timing=True)\n        end = torch.cuda.Event(enable_timing=True)\n        torch.cuda.synchronize()\n        start.record()\n        \n        if args.get_mem:\n            print(\"========== Init Data Loader ==========\")\n            init_mem = get_memory_usage(True)\n            exp_recorder.record(\"data_loader\", init_mem /\n                                GB - exp_recorder.val_dict['model_only'], 2)\n\n        # compute output\n        output = model(images)\n        loss = criterion(output, target)\n\n        if args.get_mem:\n            torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats()\n\n            print(\"========== Before Backward ==========\")\n            before_backward = get_memory_usage(True)\n            act_mem = get_memory_usage() - init_mem - \\\n                compute_tensor_bytes([loss, output])\n\n            optimizer.zero_grad()\n            loss.backward()\n            optimizer.step()\n            if args.benchmark == \"gact\":\n                def backprop():\n                    model.train()\n                    partial_bz = 8\n                    partial_image = images[:partial_bz, :, :, :]\n                    partial_target = target[:partial_bz]\n                    output = model(partial_image)\n                    loss = criterion(output, partial_target)\n                    optimizer.zero_grad()\n                    loss.backward()\n                    del loss\n                    del output\n                    del partial_image\n                    del partial_target\n                controller.iterate(backprop)\n            del loss\n\n            print(\"========== After Backward ==========\")\n            after_backward = get_memory_usage(True)\n            total_mem = before_backward + (after_backward - init_mem)\n            ref_act = before_backward - after_backward\n            peak_mem = torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated()\n            res = \"Batch size: %d\\tTotal Mem: %.2f MB\\tAct Mem: %.2f MB\\tRef Mem: %.2f\\tPeak Mem: %.2f\" % (\n                len(output), total_mem / MB, act_mem / MB, ref_act / MB, peak_mem / MB)\n            print(res)\n            exp_recorder.record(\"batch_size\", len(output))\n            exp_recorder.record(\"total\", total_mem / GB, 2)\n            exp_recorder.record(\"activation\", act_mem / GB, 2)\n            exp_recorder.dump('mem_results.json')\n            exit(0)\n\n        # measure accuracy and record loss\n        acc1, acc5 = accuracy(output, target, topk=(1, 5))\n        losses.update(loss.item(), images.size(0))\n        top1.update(acc1[0], images.size(0))\n        top5.update(acc5[0], images.size(0))\n        \n\n        # compute gradient and do SGD step\n        optimizer.zero_grad()\n        loss.backward()\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            optimizer.step()\n        \n        if i % args.print_freq == 0:\n            progress.display(i)\n        \n        # measure elapsed time\n        end.record()\n        torch.cuda.synchronize()\n        cur_batch_time = start.elapsed_time(end) / 1000.0 # event in ms\n\n        # only use 8 batch size to get sensitivity\n        def backprop():\n            model.train()\n            partial_bz = 8\n            partial_image = images[:partial_bz, :, :, :]\n            partial_target = target[:partial_bz]\n            output = model(partial_image)\n            loss = criterion(output, partial_target)\n            optimizer.zero_grad()\n            loss.backward()\n            del loss\n            del output\n            del partial_image\n            del partial_target\n        if args.benchmark == \"gact\":\n            controller.iterate(backprop)\n        bs = len(images)\n        del images\n        train_ips_list.append(bs / cur_batch_time)\n           \n        if args.get_speed:\n            global train_step_ct, train_max_batch\n            train_max_batch = max(train_max_batch, bs)\n            if train_step_ct >= 6:\n                train_ips = np.median(train_ips_list)\n                res = \"BatchSize: %d\\tIPS: %.2f\\t,Cost: %.2f ms\" % (\n                    bs, train_ips, cur_batch_time)\n                print(res, flush=True)\n                exp_recorder.record(\"network\", args.arch)\n                exp_recorder.record(\"algorithm\", args.alg)\n                exp_recorder.record(\"benchmark\", args.benchmark)\n                exp_recorder.record(\"batch_size\", train_max_batch)\n                exp_recorder.record(\"ips\", train_ips, 2)\n                exp_recorder.record(\"tstamp\", time.time(), 2)\n                exp_recorder.dump('speed_results.json')\n                exit(0)\n            train_step_ct += 1\n        \n    if args.benchmark == \"gact\":\n        controller.uninstall_hook()\n    ",
    "Experiment Result": "GACT's core mechanism involves quantizing neural network activations to reduce memory consumption, with options for adaptive precision and CPU swapping. The experimental settings explore these aspects across different machine learning models and tasks.\n\n**GACT Configuration (`gact/gact/conf.py` defaults unless specified by optimization level):**\n- `compress_activation`: Whether to enable activation compression (default: `True`).\n- `bit`: Average/default number of bits for quantization (default: 4).\n- `group_size`: Group size for quantization (default: 256).\n- `check_dup`: Avoid quantizing the same activation multiple times (default: `False`).\n- `auto_prec`: Whether automatic precision adaptation is enabled (default: `True`).\n- `max_bit`: Maximum bits allowed for auto-precision (default: 32).\n- `adapt_interval`: Number of iterations between sensitivity adaptations for auto-precision (default: 1000).\n- `work_dir`: Directory for logging debug information (default: `./log/`).\n- `log_interval`: Interval for logging debug information (default: -1, disabled).\n- `debug`: Enable debug mode for sensitivity comparison (default: `False`).\n- `swap`: Whether to offload compressed tensors to CPU (default: `False`).\n- `prefetch`: Whether to prefetch swapped tensors using separate CUDA streams (default: `False`, only effective if `swap` is `True`).\n\n**Optimization Levels (`set_optimization_level` function):**\n- `L0`: Disables activation compression (`compress_activation = False`).\n- `L1`: Fixed 4-bit quantization (`auto_prec = False`, `bit = 4`).\n- `L1.1`: Fixed 8-bit quantization (`auto_prec = False`, `bit = 8`).\n- `L1.2`: Fixed 2-bit quantization (`auto_prec = False`, `bit = 2`).\n- `L2`: Auto-precision with an average target of 4-bit (`auto_prec = True`, `bit = 4`).\n- `L2.1`: Auto-precision with an average target of 3-bit (`auto_prec = True`, `bit = 3`).\n- `L2.2`: Auto-precision with an average target of 2-bit (`auto_prec = True`, `bit = 2`).\n- `L3`: Auto-precision (4-bit average) with CPU swapping and prefetching (`auto_prec = True`, `bit = 4`, `swap = True`, `prefetch = True`).\n- `swap`: Vanilla CPU swapping without quantization (`compress_activation = False`, `swap = True`).\n- `L4bit-swap`: Fixed 4-bit quantization with CPU swapping and prefetching (`auto_prec = False`, `bit = 4`, `swap = True`, `prefetch = True`).\n\n**Integration in Benchmarks (Swin-Transformer, GNN, Text Classification, Vision):**\n- **GACT Activation:** Enabled via command-line arguments `--level` (Swin, GNN, Vision) or `--gact --opt_level` (Text Classification).\n- **Memory/Speed Profiling:** Activated by `--get-mem`, `--get-speed`, `--get-macs` flags.\n- **Gradient Checkpointing (`--use-checkpoint` in Swin-Transformer, `--ckpt` in Text Classification):** Used to save memory, and sometimes combined with GACT (e.g., `L1_ckpt` in text classification combines L1 quantization with checkpointing).\n- **Model-specific parameters:**\n    - **Swin-Transformer:** `batch-size` (varied from 16 to 800), `img_size` (224 or 299 for Inception), `network` (e.g., `swin_tiny`).\n    - **GNN:** `num-layers` (varied from 20 to 400), `hidden-size` (varied from 1280 to 12800), `model` (`gcn`, `sage`, `gat`), `num_nodes` (implicitly from dataset).\n    - **Text Classification (BERT):** `batch-size` (varied from 4 to 240, or 16 for hidden/layer search), `layer_num` (varied from 24 to 256), `hidden_size` (varied from 1280 to 5120), `intermediate_size` (varied from 30720 to 307200), `model_name_or_path` (e.g., `bert-base-cased`, `bert-large-cased`).\n    - **Vision (ResNet):** `batch-size` (varied from 32 to 1280), `input-size` (224 or 299, varied from 224 to 768), `arch` (e.g., `resnet50`, `resnet152`, `scaled_resnet_X`, `scaled_wide_resnet_Y`).\n- **Adaptation Interval (GNN):** Specifically set to 20 for sensitivity adaptation (`gact.set_adapt_interval(20)`).\n- **Metrics Recorded (`exp_recorder`):** Generally include `network`, `algorithm` (GACT level), `batch_size`/`num_nodes`, `ips` (items per second), `batch_time`, `tstamp`, and sometimes `macs`, `params`, `TFLOPS`, `total_mem`, `activation_mem`, `peak_mem`, `model_only` memory usage.\n- **Binary Search:** `exp_mem_speed.py` scripts for GNN, Text Classification, and Vision models utilize binary search to find the maximum `batch_size`, `hidden_size`, `num_layers`, `input_size`, or `width` that can fit into memory for a given GACT configuration."
}{
    "Title": "Generative Teaching Networks: Accelerating Neural Architecture Search by Learning to Generate Synthetic Training Data",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Generative Teaching Networks (GTNs), a novel meta-learning approach designed to automatically generate synthetic training data, learning environments, and curricula to accelerate AI agent learning. GTNs are shown to substantially accelerate learning, enabling learners to achieve high few-step accuracy on target tasks more rapidly than training on real data. A significant practical contribution is the acceleration of neural architecture search (NAS) evaluation (GTN-NAS), which finds higher-performing architectures for CIFAR10 with significantly reduced computational cost (up to 300x faster evaluation) compared to traditional methods and outperforms comparable weight-sharing and HyperNetwork approaches. The research also demonstrates that applying weight normalization greatly improves the stability of meta-gradient training and that learning a curriculum for data presentation enhances GTN effectiveness.",
    "Methodology": "GTNs operate with two nested training loops: an inner loop for a learner network and an outer loop for a generator network. The generator G(z,y) takes Gaussian noise (z) and a label (y) as input to produce synthetic data (x). The learner is then trained on this synthetic data for a fixed number of inner-loop SGD steps. In the outer loop, the trained learner is evaluated on real training data to compute a meta-loss. Gradients of this meta-loss are then backpropagated through the entire inner-loop learning process to update the GTN (generator and inner-loop hyperparameters). Key methodological enhancements include applying Weight Normalization to generator and learner weights to stabilize meta-gradient training, and learning a curriculum by directly optimizing the sequence of zt input vectors to the generator (instead of random sampling). Gradient-checkpointing is used to manage memory complexity. For NAS, GTNs are meta-trained on smaller 'proxy learners' to generate synthetic data for evaluating candidate architectures in the search space.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted in supervised learning domains (MNIST and CIFAR10 image classification) and a reinforcement learning domain (CartPole). For MNIST and CIFAR10, datasets were split into training, validation, and test sets. The primary evaluation metric was 'few-step accuracy' (accuracy after 32 or 128 SGD steps) for a freshly initialized learner. GTN performance was compared against baselines including training on real data and Dataset Distillation. For neural architecture search (GTN-NAS) on CIFAR10, the search space, training procedures, and hyperparameters from Neural Architecture Optimization (Luo et al., 2018) were adopted. GTN-NAS replaced the standard 'search evaluation' training (smaller architectures, 100 epochs on real data) with 'GTN evaluation' (synthetic data) to achieve significant speedups. GTNs were meta-trained for 8 hours on a p6000 GPU using proxy learners. Spearman rank correlation was used to assess how well GTN evaluation predicted the performance of architectures. Final architectures found by GTN-NAS were evaluated after 600 epochs. Experiments were typically run 5 times, reporting mean and 95% confidence intervals from bootstrapping.",
    "Limitations": "The initial experiments primarily focused on supervised learning, with a single simple reinforcement learning task (CartPole) explored later. Optimization through meta-gradients without weight normalization was found to be unstable and highly sensitive to hyperparameters, often leading to divergence. Training GTNs directly on complex NAS architectures proved prohibitively slow, requiring meta-training on smaller proxy learners instead, and then relying on generalization. The synthetic images generated by GTNs, especially for CIFAR10, were often unrealistic and unrecognizable, despite being effective for training. While GTNs enable rapid few-step learning, they are not expected to produce state-of-the-art (SOTA) asymptotic accuracy compared to traditional methods training extensively on full real datasets, partly due to data compression and generalization across diverse architectures. When more inner-loop optimization steps are available, training on real data eventually surpasses synthetic GTN data. The GTN-NAS method, as tested, used random search for architecture proposals, which is less sophisticated than some other advanced NAS techniques.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include leveraging GTNs to create neural networks on-demand that meet specific design constraints (e.g., accuracy, inference time, memory requirements) or possess unique skill sets. Further investigation into the broader applicability of weight normalization for stabilizing other meta-gradient-based techniques (e.g., MAML, learned optimizers, learned update rules) is suggested. Combining GTNs with existing NAS techniques like weight sharing, Graph HyperNetworks, or more intelligent architecture proposal algorithms (e.g., NAO) could yield further performance gains. Extending GTNs to unsupervised learning, for instance, by training useful embedding functions, is another promising avenue. GTNs could also be applied to stabilize GAN training and prevent mode collapse. A particularly interesting extension is introducing closed-loop curricula, where a recurrent GTN dynamically adapts its generated samples based on the learner's progress, similar to a human tutor. Finally, GTNs could be developed to generate entire training environments (e.g., synthetic Markov Decision Processes) for reinforcement learning agents, potentially enabling the creation of AI-generating algorithms (AI-GAs) that continuously generate new, complex learning challenges and even produce recognizable, human-meaningful virtual worlds.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Accelerating Transformer Pre-training with 2:4 Sparsity",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the problem of slow transformer pre-training by leveraging NVIDIA Ampere GPU's 2:4 sparse matrix multiplication capability. It proposes an end-to-end acceleration method for transformers, introducing three accuracy-preserving techniques (masked decay on gradients, fast decay factor determination, dense fine-tuning at the end of pre-training) and two acceleration techniques (convolution-based transposable mask computation, L2 cache-miss reduction for gated activations).     "Methodology": "The core methodology is Fully Sparse Training (FST) on Feed-Forward Networks (FFNs) of transformers using 2:4 semi-structured sparsity. It employs transposable N:M masks and a Minimum-Variance Unbiased Estimator (MVUE) for gradient pruning. Accuracy is preserved by introducing a \"flip rate\" stability metric, applying masked decay on gradients (rather than weights) for Adam-like optimizers, devising a fast test-based method to determine the decay factor λW during warm-up, and incorporating a dense fine-tuning phase at the end of pre-training. Training acceleration is achieved through a convolution-based approach for faster transposable mask computation and a custom kernel for gated activation functions (e.g., GEGLU) optimized for column-major memory access to reduce GPU L2 cache misses. Mask updates are also batched to reduce overhead. CUTLASS and Triton are used for kernel implementations.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on NVIDIA RTX3090 GPUs using FP16 mixed precision. Evaluation metrics included GLUE scores (Avg, COLA, MNLI, MRPC, QNLI, QQP, RTE, SST-2, STS-B, WNLI) and SQuAD scores for language models, ACC@1 and ACC@5 for vision models, and BLEU score for machine translation. Speedups were measured for FFN layers, transformer blocks, and end-to-end training, comparing against dense baselines, \"Half\" BERT, STEP, and Bi-Mask. An ablation study was performed on BERT-base to assess individual contributions of proposed techniques.",
    "Limitations": "The paper highlights that direct application of existing 2:4 sparse training methods (developed for CNNs) to transformers resulted in low accuracy and practical inefficiency. The determination of the optimal masked decay factor (λW) is non-trivial and varies significantly across transformer models, making grid searching impractical. The achieved end-to-end acceleration of 1.2x, while significant, is less than the theoretical 2x speedup of 2:4-spMM due to other operations like pruning overhead and activation functions. The method specifically leverages hardware features of NVIDIA Ampere GPUs, implying potential limited portability to other architectures. The authors also mention the potential negative societal impact if used to accelerate malicious software.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper implicitly suggests exploring methods to achieve higher end-to-end acceleration, potentially by optimizing more components beyond FFNs. Further research could focus on generalizing the fast decay factor determination method to be more adaptive. While not explicitly stated as future work, the impact statement's mention of accelerating large models like GLM and LLaMA implies continued application and refinement of the sparse training techniques to a broader range of state-of-the-art transformers.",

    "Title": "Transformer Quality in Linear Time",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the quadratic complexity of Transformers for long sequences, which limits their context size. It proposes FLASH, a new model family that achieves linear scalability over context size on modern accelerators while matching the quality (perplexity) of fully augmented Transformers. Key contributions include the Gated Attention Unit (GAU) layer, which simplifies attention and reduces its quality dependence, and a novel mixed chunk attention method that combines local quadratic attention within chunks and global linear attention across chunks for practical efficiency.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology involves a two-step approach: 1) Designing the Gated Attention Unit (GAU) layer, which integrates a gating mechanism (similar to GLU) with a simpler, single-head, softmax-free attention mechanism. This makes the layer more amenable to approximation. 2) Proposing 'mixed chunk attention' to approximate the quadratic attention in GAU. This method chunks the input sequence into non-overlapping segments, applying precise quadratic attention within each chunk and fast linear attention across chunks. The linear attention across chunks uses a cumulative sum at the chunk level, significantly speeding up auto-regressive training by reducing sequential dependency from T to T/C steps. The attention kernel uses squared ReLU, and relative positional embeddings (RoPE) are incorporated.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on bidirectional (masked) and auto-regressive language modeling tasks. Validation metrics were negative log perplexity (for LM/MLM) and F1 score (for fine-tuning on TriviaQA). Baselines included vanilla Transformer, Transformer+ (with RoPE), Transformer++ (with RoPE + GLU), Performer, and Combiner. Hardware used was TPU-v4 cores and Nvidia V100 GPUs. Ablation studies investigated the impact of quadratic/linear components, GAU, and chunk size.",
    "Limitations": "The paper acknowledges that while overlapping local attention (like in Longformer/BigBird) could improve quality, it introduces memory re-formatting operations that harm actual running speed, making the cost-benefit trade-off less favorable than simply adding more layers. The current work also does not investigate the scaling laws of the new model family or extensively explore its performance on a wide array of downstream tasks, beyond a single fine-tuning experiment on TriviaQA.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work includes investigating the scaling laws of the FLASH model family and further exploring its performance on a broader range of downstream tasks.     "Title": "SAC: Accelerating and Structuring Self-Attention via Sparse Adaptive Connection",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Sparse Adaptive Connection (SAC) to address the quadratic memory complexity and fixed attention structure of traditional self-attention mechanisms. SAC regards the input sequence as a graph and learns to dynamically construct sparse attention edges between salient nodes using an LSTM-based edge predictor, optimized with reinforcement learning. This approach significantly reduces memory costs while maintaining or improving task-specific performances. The work also demonstrates that various existing self-attention models are special cases of SAC, highlighting its generality. Experimental results show SAC is competitive with state-of-the-art models across neural machine translation, language modeling, graph representation learning, and image classification.",
    "Methodology": "SAC employs an LSTM Edge Predictor to construct sparse connections for self-attention. The predictor sequentially identifies pairs of original and destination nodes to form edges, with the total number of edges controlled by a hyperparameter α. Node representations are updated based on self-attention applied only to these linked nodes. To incorporate inherent structural information (e.g., relative distances in text, graph edges), distance encodings are added to the projection matrix during destination node prediction. The Edge Predictor is trained using REINFORCE, a policy gradient method, maximizing a reward signal derived from the downstream task's log-likelihood. At test time, edges are decoded using beam search. Variants include enforcing the same edge structure across all layers (to reduce computational cost and RL search space) and a head-adaptive strategy where different attention heads have their own edge predictors.",
    "Experimental Setup": "SAC was evaluated on four diverse tasks. For Neural Machine Translation, an encoder-decoder model was used with WMT 2014 English-German dataset (4.5M sentence pairs, BPE tokens), optimized with Adam, label smoothing, and evaluated by BLEU scores. For Language Modeling, character-level Enwiki8 and Text8 datasets were used with a Transformer decoder, optimized with Adagrad, and evaluated by Bits Per Character (BPC). Graph Representation Learning involved transductive (Cora, Citeseer, Pubmed) and inductive (PPI) benchmarks, using a GAT model for initialization, Adam optimizer, early stopping, and evaluated by classification accuracy or micro-F1. For Image Classification, CIFAR-100 and ImageNet datasets were used, augmenting Wide-ResNet-28-10 and ResNet-50 backbones respectively with self-attention, and evaluated by Top-1 and Top-5 accuracy. Hyperparameter α (number of edges per node) was varied across tasks (e.g., 2-15 for NMT, 256 for LM, 5-100 for graph, 5-20 for image).",
    "Limitations": "The head-adaptive strategy, which allows different attention heads to attend to different contexts, did not consistently provide significant performance boosts across all experimental settings and substantially increased the reinforcement learning search space. The authors adopted a strategy where all attention layers share the same edge structure to reduce the computational cost and RL search space, implying that learning distinct structures per layer is more complex and potentially prohibitive. The use of REINFORCE for training the edge predictor, as a policy gradient method, typically entails sensitivities to hyper-parameters and training stability, potentially requiring careful tuning.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors state that SAC has great potential to generate high-quality long text, addressing the limitations of vanilla self-attention models in processing extremely long inputs without adequate context. This implies future work could focus on applying SAC specifically to long-form text generation tasks. Further research could also explore more advanced reinforcement learning techniques or reward functions for optimizing the edge predictor, potentially allowing for more flexible or complex adaptive attention patterns. Investigating when and why the head-adaptive strategy is beneficial could also be a fruitful avenue.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "O(n) Connections are Expressive Enough: Universal Approximability of Sparse Transformers",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses fundamental questions about sparse Transformers: their ability to universally approximate sequence-to-sequence functions and how sparsity patterns and levels affect performance. It proposes a unified framework for sparse attention models and proves sufficient conditions (Assumptions 1 and 2) under which sparse Transformers are universal approximators. A key finding is that sparse Transformers with only O(n) connections per attention layer can approximate the same function class as dense models with n^2 connections. The work also presents experimental comparisons of different sparsity patterns and levels on standard NLP tasks.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology involves a theoretical analysis and an empirical study. For the theoretical part, a unified framework for sparse Transformers is introduced, which modifies the standard Transformer block to include cycling sparsity patterns ({Al k}) and a generalized probability map (ρ) instead of softmax. Two assumptions are proposed: Assumption 1 defines conditions on sparsity patterns (token attends to itself, a Hamiltonian path of direct connections, and all tokens reachable in 's' hops), and Assumption 2 defines conditions on the probability map (approximates hardmax for inputs with sufficient margin). The universal approximation theorem is proven in three steps: approximating the target function with a piecewise constant function, exactly representing it with a modified sparse Transformer (using hardmax and piecewise linear activations), and then approximating this modified model with a standard sparse Transformer (using ρ and ReLU), while carefully controlling error accumulation. This involves adapting 'selective shift' and 'all-max-shift' operations for sparse settings. The empirical part compares different sparsity patterns (STRIDED, FIXED, STAR, RANDOM) and head configurations (SEQUENTIAL, UNION, MULTIHEAD).",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on four tasks: a synthetic copying task (0s0s format, 127-length sequences, max n=256), language modeling (One Billion Word Benchmark, max n=256), translation (WMT18 English-Czech, tested on Newstest 2015; also WMT English-German/German-English, max n=256), and GLUE tasks (MNLI, XNLI, CoLA, MRPC using BERTBASE, max n=128). Models used either one sparse Transformer block (copying task) or multiple blocks (12 for LM, 6 for translation, BERTBASE for GLUE). The number of attention heads (h), head size (m), hidden layer size (r), and embedding dimension (d) varied by task. Optimizers included AdamW (copying) and Adafactor (LM/translation). For GLUE, a BERTBASE model was pre-trained on BooksCorpus and English Wikipedia, then fine-tuned. Performance was measured by prediction accuracy, perplexity, and BLEU score.",
    "Limitations": "The universal approximation theorem (Theorem 1) views the sequence length 'n' as a fixed constant and does not apply to varying sequence lengths. The current theoretical analysis primarily applies to the encoder part of the Transformer network. Experimentally, the study focuses on tasks with small sequence lengths, thus complementing but not fully replacing existing work on long sequence tasks. Specific patterns showed limitations; for instance, the STAR pattern performed poorly on the copying task due to its local attention combined with the task's requirement for copying distant tokens. The experiments also suggested that the optimal sparsity pattern design is highly task-dependent, with different patterns/configurations excelling in different tasks.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper aims to shed light on sparsity in attention layers and guide the design of sparse attention models, suggesting avenues for further exploration. Future research could focus on extending the universal approximation theorem to Transformers with varying sequence lengths (beyond a fixed 'n'). Additionally, the analysis could be extended to the decoder parts of Transformer networks. Given the task-dependent performance of sparsity patterns observed in experiments, more research is needed to develop principled guidance for designing optimal sparsity patterns for diverse tasks. Further investigation into other sparsity patterns and probability maps that satisfy the proposed theoretical conditions would also be beneficial.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Learning to Reconstruct Missing Data from Spatiotemporal Graphs with Sparse Observations",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces a novel attention-based architecture, named Spatiotemporal Point Inference Network (SPIN), designed to reconstruct missing data points (impute) in multivariate time series modeled as spatiotemporal graphs with sparse observations. The key contribution is a sparse spatiotemporal attention mechanism that learns localized representations by exploiting a propagation architecture aligned with the imputation task. Unlike autoregressive methods, SPIN handles highly sparse data without propagating prediction errors or requiring bidirectional models. Empirical results demonstrate its superior effectiveness, especially in highly sparse settings, outperforming state-of-the-art imputation methods.",
    "Methodology": "The proposed method, SPIN, is a graph attention network for multivariate time series imputation. It learns representations for discrete points in time and space by aggregating information from available observations at neighboring nodes, weighted by input-dependent attention coefficients. The core component is a novel sparse spatiotemporal attention layer that performs both inter-node spatiotemporal cross-attention (aggregating from neighbors) and intra-node temporal self-attention (aggregating from the node's own observed history). Representations are initialized using MLPs on spatiotemporal coordinates (and observations if available) and then updated through stacked attention layers. A two-phase propagation mechanism allows for initial information diffusion through masked connections, which are later unlocked. To mitigate computational complexity (O((Nmax + Emax)T^2)), two approaches are proposed: graph subsampling during training and a hierarchical attention mechanism (SPIN-H) that introduces dummy hub nodes to reduce complexity to O((Nmax + Emax)KT). Spatiotemporal positional encodings combine sine/cosine transforms for temporal features and learnable parameters for spatial features.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on three real-world datasets: PEMS-BAY and METR-LA (trafﬁc benchmarks) and AQI (air quality stations in China), including a smaller version, AQI-36. Missing data was introduced using two policies for traffic datasets: 'Block missing' (5% random mask plus simulated failures) and 'Point missing' (25% random drop). AQI datasets used their natural missing data distribution (25.67% to ~36%). The adjacency matrix for graphs was derived from sensor pairwise geographical distances using a thresholded Gaussian kernel. Performance was measured using Mean Absolute Error (MAE) in an out-of-sample scenario. Baselines included GRIN (state-of-the-art GNN), Transformer, SAITS, BRITS, rGAIN, and simpler methods like MEAN, KNN, MF, MICE, and VAR. SPIN models were trained using Adam optimizer, a cosine scheduler, and a loss function that computes absolute error across all layers. An ablation study was conducted to assess the contribution of cross-attention and self-attention components.",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitation of the standard SPIN model is its computational complexity (O((Nmax + Emax)T^2)), which restricts its application to large graphs and long time horizons, although the SPIN-H variant addresses this by introducing an information bottleneck. In extremely sparse training settings, SPIN may suffer from a lack of supervision, potentially slowing down the learning process.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on addressing the 'lack of supervision' issue in extremely sparse training settings by introducing additional auxiliary learning tasks. Another promising direction is to further investigate spatiotemporal positional encoding methods and their optimized application within sensor networks.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch\nfrom torch import nn, Tensor\nfrom torch.nn import LayerNorm\nfrom torch_geometric.typing import OptTensor\nfrom tsl.nn.base import StaticGraphEmbedding\nfrom tsl.nn.blocks.encoders import MLP\n\nfrom ..layers import PositionalEncoder, TemporalGraphAdditiveAttention\n\n\nclass SPINModel(nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, input_size: int,\n                 hidden_size: int,\n                 n_nodes: int,\n                 u_size: Optional[int] = None,\n                 output_size: Optional[int] = None,\n                 temporal_self_attention: bool = True,\n                 reweight: Optional[str] = 'softmax',\n                 n_layers: int = 4,\n                 eta: int = 3,\n                 message_layers: int = 1):\n        super(SPINModel, self).__init__()\n\n        u_size = u_size or input_size\n        output_size = output_size or input_size\n        self.n_nodes = n_nodes\n        self.n_layers = n_layers\n        self.eta = eta\n        self.temporal_self_attention = temporal_self_attention\n\n        self.u_enc = PositionalEncoder(in_channels=u_size,\n                                       out_channels=hidden_size,\n                                       n_layers=2,\n                                       n_nodes=n_nodes)\n\n        self.h_enc = MLP(input_size, hidden_size, n_layers=2)\n        self.h_norm = LayerNorm(hidden_size)\n\n        self.valid_emb = StaticGraphEmbedding(n_nodes, hidden_size)\n        self.mask_emb = StaticGraphEmbedding(n_nodes, hidden_size)\n\n        self.x_skip = nn.ModuleList()\n        self.encoder, self.readout = nn.ModuleList(), nn.ModuleList()\n        for l in range(n_layers):\n            x_skip = nn.Linear(input_size, hidden_size)\n            encoder = TemporalGraphAdditiveAttention(\n                input_size=hidden_size,\n                output_size=hidden_size,\n                msg_size=hidden_size,\n                msg_layers=message_layers,\n                temporal_self_attention=temporal_self_attention,\n                reweight=reweight,\n                mask_temporal=True,\n                mask_spatial=l < eta,\n                norm=True,\n                root_weight=True,\n                dropout=0.0\n            )\n            readout = MLP(hidden_size, hidden_size, output_size,\n                          n_layers=2)\n            self.x_skip.append(x_skip)\n            self.encoder.append(encoder)\n            self.readout.append(readout)\n\n    def forward(self, x: Tensor, u: Tensor, mask: Tensor,\n                edge_index: Tensor, edge_weight: OptTensor = None,\n                node_index: OptTensor = None, target_nodes: OptTensor = None):\n        if target_nodes is None:\n            target_nodes = slice(None)\n\n        # Whiten missing values\n        x = x * mask\n\n        # POSITIONAL ENCODING #################################################\n        # Obtain spatio-temporal positional encoding for every node-step pair #\n        # in both observed and target sets. Encoding are obtained by jointly  #\n        # processing node and time positional encoding.                       #\n\n        # Build (node, timestamp) encoding\n        q = self.u_enc(u, node_index=node_index)\n        # Condition value on key\n        h = self.h_enc(x) + q\n\n        # ENCODER #############################################################\n        # Obtain representations h^i_t for every (i, t) node-step pair by     #\n        # only taking into account valid data in representation set.          #\n\n        # Replace H in missing entries with queries Q\n        h = torch.where(mask.bool(), h, q)\n        # Normalize features\n        h = self.h_norm(h)\n\n        imputations = []\n\n        for l in range(self.n_layers):\n            if l == self.eta:\n                # Condition H on two different embeddings to distinguish\n                # valid values from masked ones\n                valid = self.valid_emb(token_index=node_index)\n                masked = self.mask_emb(token_index=node_index)\n                h = torch.where(mask.bool(), h + valid, h + masked)\n            # Masked Temporal GAT for encoding representation\n            h = h + self.x_skip[l](x) * mask  # skip connection for valid x\n            h = self.encoder[l](h, edge_index, mask=mask)\n            # Read from H to get imputations\n            target_readout = self.readout[l](h[..., target_nodes, :])\n            imputations.append(target_readout)\n\n        # Get final layer imputations\n        x_hat = imputations.pop(-1)\n\n        return x_hat, imputations\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def add_model_specific_args(parser):\n        parser.opt_list('--hidden-size', type=int, tunable=True, default=32,\n                        options=[32, 64, 128, 256])\n        parser.add_argument('--u-size', type=int, default=None)\n        parser.add_argument('--output-size', type=int, default=None)\n        parser.add_argument('--temporal-self-attention', type=bool,\n                            default=True)\n        parser.add_argument('--reweight', type=str, default='softmax')\n        parser.add_argument('--n-layers', type=int, default=4)\n        parser.add_argument('--eta', type=int, default=3)\n        parser.add_argument('--message-layers', type=int, default=1)\n        return parser",

}{
    "Title": "Even Sparser Graph Transformers",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the quadratic memory complexity of Graph Transformers, which limits their scalability to large graphs, even with sparse attention variants like Exphormer. It proposes Spexphormer, a two-stage procedure that leverages the consistency of attention scores across network widths to drastically reduce memory requirements. Key contributions include: 1) Experimental and theoretical analysis of attention score similarity for networks of different widths, with architectural changes (V normalization, variable temperature) to improve this similarity. 2) Introduction of layer-wise sparsification using reservoir sampling based on learned attention scores, with theoretical guarantees. 3) A two-phase training process that achieves competitive accuracy while significantly reducing memory consumption, enabling scaling to larger datasets. 4) Enables novel batching techniques and efficient regular matrix calculations due to fixed node degrees.",
    "Methodology": "Spexphormer employs a two-phase training process. The first phase trains an 'Attention Score Estimator Network,' a narrow-width network (e.g., 4 or 8 hidden dimensions, one attention head) on a high-degree expander graph (30 to 200). This network's purpose is to estimate pairwise attention score patterns. It includes two architectural additions: normalization of rows of V with a learnable global scale and a variable temperature parameter that anneals from 1.0 to 0.05 to sharpen attention scores. The learned attention scores from this narrow network are then used in the second phase to construct sparse, layer-wise interaction graphs for a wider, final network. This sparsification selects a fixed number of edges per attention layer (degℓ) for each node using GPU-friendly, parallel reservoir sampling. The fixed degree enables efficient regular matrix calculations. Batching for large graphs is achieved through neighborhood sampling based on these learned attention scores. The method is supported by theoretical underpinnings showing that a narrow network's attention scores can approximate a wide network's scores and that the sampling procedure can form a sparse matrix that approximates the full attention matrix.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on twelve medium-sized graphs (six homophilic: CS, Physics, Photo, Computer, WikiCS, ogbn-arxiv; and six heterophilic: Minesweeper, Tolokers, Roman-empire, Amazon-ratings, Questions, Actor) and three large graph datasets (ogbn-proteins, Amazon2M, Pokec). Evaluation metrics included accuracy for most datasets and ROC-AUC for Minesweeper, Tolokers, Questions, and ogbn-proteins. Data splits followed standard practices or previous work: 60%/20%/20% random splits, 50%/25%/25% for Actor, and various splits for large graphs. Baselines included GCN, GraphSAGE, GAT, GraphSAINT, Nodeformer, Difformer, SGFormer, GraphGPS, GOAT, GloGNN, SGC, NAGphormer, Exphormer, and SIGN. Attention score estimation was analyzed on Actor and Photo datasets across hidden dimensions from 4 to 64, using energy distance, entropy, Precision, Recall, Density, Coverage, and top-k attention sum, with and without expander/self-loop edges. Hardware included A100 (40GB) and V100 (32GB, 16GB) GPUs for training, and Intel Xeon E5-2680 v4 CPU with 500GB RAM for large graph attention score estimation. Hyperparameters were tuned and detailed in tables for different datasets.",
    "Limitations": "The method, while successfully scaling to datasets with over two million nodes, relies on substantial CPU memory (up to 500GB in experiments) for the attention score estimation phase on extremely large datasets. This implies that for truly massive datasets beyond the tested scale, the initial attention score estimation might still be infeasible without highly distributed computing resources.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A potential future research direction is to explore combining the sampling process with simultaneous attention score estimation in a dynamic manner. This would allow for scaling the attention score estimation itself to even larger graphs, addressing the current limitation of high CPU memory requirements for this initial phase.",
    "Experiment Code": "File Path: spexphormer/config/data_preprocess_config.py\nContent:\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.register import register_config\nfrom yacs.config import CfgNode as CN\n\n\ndef nn\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.config import cfg\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.register import register_layer\n\n\ndef separate_by_node(edges, scores):\n    num_nodes = edges.max().item() + 1\n    neighbors = [[] for _ in range(num_nodes)]\n    neighbors_edge_idx = [[] for _ in range(num_nodes)]\n    P = [[] for _ in range(num_nodes)]\n    sum_scores = torch.zeros(num_nodes, dtype=scores.dtype)\n    rev_sum_scores = torch.zeros(num_nodes, dtype=scores.dtype)\n    \n    for i in range(edges.size(1)):\n        u, v = edges[:, i]\n        neighbors[v].append(u.item())\n        neighbors_edge_idx[v].append(i)\n        w = scores[i].item()\n        sum_scores[v] += w\n        rev_sum_scores[u] += w\n        P[v].append(w)\n\n    return neighbors, neighbors_edge_idx, P, sum_scores.tolist(), rev_sum_scores.tolist()\n\n\nclass ASE_Attention_Layer(nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, num_heads, layer_idx, use_bias=False, dim_edge=None, use_virt_nodes=False):\n        super().__init__()\n\n        self.out_dim = out_dim // num_heads\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.use_virt_nodes = use_virt_nodes\n        self.use_bias = use_bias\n        self.layer_idx = layer_idx\n\n        if dim_edge is None:\n            dim_edge = in_dim\n\n        self.QKV = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads * 3, bias=use_bias)\n        self.V_scale = nn.Parameter(data=torch.Tensor([0.25]), requires_grad=True)\n\n        self.use_edge_attr = cfg.gt.use_edge_feats\n        if self.use_edge_attr:\n            self.E1 = nn.Linear(dim_edge, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n            self.E2 = nn.Linear(dim_edge, num_heads, bias=True)\n\n        self.T = 1.0\n\n    def propagate_attention(self, batch, edge_index):\n        src = batch.K_h[edge_index[0].to(torch.long)]  # (num edges) x num_heads x out_dim\n        dest = batch.Q_h[edge_index[1].to(torch.long)]  # (num edges) x num_heads x out_dim\n        score = torch.einsum('ehd,ehd->eh', src, dest)  # Efficient batch matrix multiplication\n\n        # Scale scores by sqrt(d)\n        score = score / np.sqrt(self.out_dim)\n\n        if self.use_edge_attr:\n            score = score * batch.E.sum(-1)  # (num real edges) x num_heads\n            score = score.unsqueeze(-1) + batch.E2\n        else:\n            score = score.unsqueeze(-1)\n\n        score = score / self.T\n        score = torch.exp(score.clamp(-8, 8))\n\n\n        # Apply attention score to each source node to create edge messages\n        msg = batch.V_h[edge_index[0].to(torch.long)] * score  # (num real edges) x num_heads x out_dim\n        \n        # Add-up real msgs in destination nodes as given by batch.edge_index[1]\n        batch.wV = torch.zeros_like(batch.V_h)  # (num nodes in batch) x num_heads x out_dim\n        batch.wV.index_add_(0, edge_index[1].to(torch.long), msg)  # Using index_add_ as an alternative to scatter\n\n        # Compute attention normalization coefficient\n        batch.Z = score.new_zeros(batch.V_h.size(0), self.num_heads, 1)  # (num nodes in batch) x num_heads x 1\n        batch.Z.index_add_(0, edge_index[1].to(torch.long), score)  # Using index_add_ as an alternative to scatter\n\n        if cfg.train.saving_epoch:\n            new_score = score/(batch.Z[edge_index[1]])\n            score_np = new_score.cpu().detach().numpy()\n            Path(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}').mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n            with open(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}/seed{cfg.seed}_h{self.out_dim}_layer_{self.layer_idx}.npy', 'wb') as f:\n                np.save(f, score_np)\n\n\n    def forward(self, batch):\n        if cfg.train.cur_epoch >= cfg.train.temp_wait:\n            self.T = max(cfg.train.temp_min, cfg.train.temp_rdc_ratio ** (cfg.train.cur_epoch - cfg.train.temp_wait))\n        edge_attr = batch.edge_attr\n        edge_index = batch.edge_index\n        h = batch.x\n        num_node = batch.batch.shape[0]\n        \n        QKV_h = self.QKV(h).view(-1, self.num_heads, 3 * self.out_dim)\n        batch.Q_h, batch.K_h, batch.V_h = torch.split(QKV_h, self.out_dim, dim=-1)\n        batch.V_h = F.normalize(batch.V_h, p=2.0, dim=-1) * self.V_scale\n\n        if self.use_edge_attr:\n            if cfg.dataset.edge_encoder_name == 'TypeDictEdge2':\n                E = self.E1(batch.edge_embeddings)[batch.edge_attr]\n                E2 = self.E2(batch.edge_embeddings)[batch.edge_attr]\n            else:\n                E = self.E1(edge_attr)\n                E2 = self.E2(edge_attr)\n            \n            batch.E = E.view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n            batch.E2 = E2.view(-1, self.num_heads, 1)\n\n        self.propagate_attention(batch, edge_index)\n\n        # Normalize the weighted sum of values by the normalization coefficient\n        h_out = batch.wV / (batch.Z + 1e-6)\n\n        # Reshape the output to combine the heads\n        h_out = h_out.view(-1, self.out_dim * self.num_heads)\n\n        # Separate virtual node embeddings if virtual nodes are used\n        if self.use_virt_nodes:\n            batch.virt_h = h_out[num_node:]\n            h_out = h_out[:num_node]\n\n        return h_out\n\n\nregister_layer('ASE_Attention_Layer', ASE_Attention_Layer)\n\nFile Path: dividable by the number of heads')\n        self.out_dim = out_dim // num_heads\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.layer_idx = layer_idx\n\n        self.edge_index_name = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n        self.E1 = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n        self.E2 = nn.Linear(in_dim, num_heads, bias=True)\n        self.V = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n\n    def forward(self, batch):\n        edge_index = getattr(batch, self.edge_index_name)\n        edge_attr = getattr(batch, self.edge_attr_name)\n        \n        n1 = batch.num_layer_nodes[self.layer_idx].item()\n        n2 = batch.num_layer_nodes[self.layer_idx + 1].item()\n        assert batch.x.shape[0] == n1\n\n        Q_h = self.Q(batch.x[:n2]).view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n        K_h = self.K(batch.x).view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n        V_h = self.V(batch.x).view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n\n        if cfg.dataset.edge_encoder_name == 'TypeDictEdge2':\n            E1 = self.E1(batch.edge_embeddings)[edge_attr].view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n            E2 = self.E2(batch.edge_embeddings)[edge_attr].view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, 1)\n        else:\n            E1 = self.E1(edge_attr).view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n            E2 = self.E2(edge_attr).view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, 1)\n        \n        neighbors = edge_index[0, :]\n        deg = neighbors.shape[0]//n2\n        neighbors = neighbors.reshape(n2, deg)\n        \n        K_h = K_h[neighbors]\n        V_h = V_h[neighbors]\n        \n        score = torch.mul(E1, K_h)\n        \n        score = torch.bmm(score.view(-1, deg, self.out_dim), Q_h.view(-1, self.out_dim, 1))\n        score = score.view(-1, self.num_heads, deg)\n\n        score = score + E2.squeeze(-1).permute([0, 2, 1])\n        score = score.clamp(-8, 8)\n        score = F.softmax(score, dim=-1)\n        \n        V_h = V_h.permute(0, 2, 1, 3)\n        score = score.unsqueeze(-1)\n        h_out = torch.mul(score, V_h)\n        h_out = h_out.sum(dim=2)\n        h_out = h_out.reshape(n2, -1)\n\n        return h_out\n\nregister_layer('ExphormerFinal', SpexphormerAttention)\nregister_layer('ExphormerSecond', SpexphormerAttention)\nregister_layer('ExphormerRegularGraph', SpexphormerAttention)\n\nFile Path: spexphormer/network/SpExphormer_model.py\nContent:\nimport torch\nimport torch_geometric.graphgym.register as register\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.config import pandas as pd\nimport torch\nfrom torch_geometric.data import Data\nfrom bottleneck import argpartition\n\ndef sampler_uniform(adj_eidx, P, P_inv, k):\n    deg = adj_eidx.shape[0]\n    if k > deg:\n        extra = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k - deg, replace=True)\n        return np.concatenate([adj_eidx, extra])\n    idx = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k, replace=False)\n    return idx\n\ndef sampler_reservoir(adj_eidx, P, P_inv, k):\n    deg = adj_eidx.shape[0]\n    if k > deg:\n        extra = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k - deg, replace=True, p=P)\n        return np.concatenate([adj_eidx, extra])\n    rsv = -np.log(np.random.rand(deg)) * P_inv\n    idx = argpartition(rsv, k-1)[:k]\n    return adj_eidx[idx]\n\n# The maximum can be cached for each node to not repeat this process. Caching is not implemented here.\ndef sampler_get_max(adj_eidx, P, P_inv, k):\n    deg = adj_eidx.shape[0]\n    if k > deg:\n        extra = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k - deg, replace=True, p=P)\n        return np.concatenate([adj_eidx, extra])\n    idx = argpartition(P, P.shape[0]-k)[P.shape[0]-k:]\n    return adj_eidx[idx]\n\n\nclass NeighborSampler():\n    def __init__(self, original_graph, edge_index, edge_attr, P, adj_eidx, deg, num_layers, sampler='graph_reservoir') -> None:\n        \"\"\"\n        Initialize the NeighborSampler.\n        Parameters:\n        original_graph (torch_geometric.data.Data): The original graph containing n nodes and m edges.\n        edge_index (torch.Tensor): Tensor of shape (2, m) representing the edge indices.\n        edge_attr (torch.Tensor): Tensor of shape (m, 1) representing the edge attributes.\n        P (list): List of length n, where each element is a 2D numpy array of size (num_layers, d_i). \n                  P[i][l][j] represents the attention score of the j-th neighbor of node i in layer l.\n        adj_eidx (list): List of length n, where adj_eidx[i][j] is the index of the edge between node i \n                         and its j-th neighbor in the edge_index.\n        deg (list): List representing the number of neighbors to sample per layer.\n        num_layers (int): Number of layers in the model.\n        sampler (str): Sampling strategy. It consists of two parts: the first part indicates whether \n                       sampling happens on the graph first and then batching is done, and the second \n                       part indicates the strategy of sampling. Default is 'graph_reservoir'.\n        Returns:\n        None\n        \"\"\"\n\n        self.data = original_graph\n        self.edge_index, self.edge_attr, self.P, self.adj_eidx, self.deg, in calculations\n        self.P_inv = tuple([1.0/(p+1e-8) for p in self.P])\n\n        if self.sampler in ['graph_reservoir', 'graph_uniform']:\n            self.sample_on_batching = False\n            self.sampled_edge_ids = []\n            self.all_nodes_neighbor_sampler()\n        else:\n            self.sample_on_batching = True\n\n    \n    def expand_neighborhood_layer(self, layer_nodes, layer_idx):\n        k = self.deg[layer_idx]\n        \n        if self.sample_on_batching:\n            if self.sampler == 'batch_reservoir':\n                layer_edge_ids = [sampler_reservoir(self.adj_eidx[v], self.P[v][layer_idx], self.P_inv[v][layer_idx], k) for v in layer_nodes]\n            elif self.sampler == 'batch_uniform':\n                layer_edge_ids = self.edge_index[:, layer_edge_ids]\n\n        new_nodes = layer_edge_index[0, :].squeeze()\n        new_layer_nodes = np.concatenate((layer_nodes, new_nodes))\n        new_layer_nodes = core_nodes):\n        edges = []\n        nodes = []\n        edge_ids = []\n        layer_nodes = np.array(core_nodes)\n        for layer_idx in reversed(range(self.num_layers)):\n edge_ids.append(layer_edge_ids)\n\n        edges = edges[::-1]\n        nodes = nodes[::-1]\n        edge_ids = edge_ids[::-1]\n        data = Data(x=self.data.x[nodes[0]])\n\n        max_idx = np.max(nodes[0])\n        index_mapping = torch.zeros(max_idx+1, dtype=torch.int)\n        index_mapping[nodes[0]] = torch.arange(nodes[0].shape[0], dtype=torch.int)\n        for l in This list is symmetric; i.e., if\n  (x, y) is an edge so is (y,x).\n  Args:\n    num_nodes: Number of nodes in the desired graph.\n    degree: Desired degree.\n    rng: random number generator\n  Returns:\n    senders: tail of each edge.\n    receivers: head of each edge.\n  \"\"\"\n\n  if rng is None:\n    rng = np.random.default_rng()\n\n  senders = []\n  receivers = []\n  for _ in range(degree):\n    permutation = rng.permutation(list(range(num_nodes))).tolist()\n    for idx, v in enumerate(permutation):\n      u = permutation[idx - 1]\n      senders.extend([v, u])\n      receivers.extend([u, v])\n\n  senders = np.array(senders)\n  receivers = np.array(receivers)\n\n  return senders, receivers\n\n\ndef augment_with_expander(data, degree, algorithm, rng=None, max_num_iters=100, exp_index=0):\n  \"\"\"Generates a random d-regular expander graph with n nodes.\n  Returns the list of edges.     "Experiment Result": "Phase 1: Attention Score Estimator Network Settings\n- `cfg.gt.layer_type`: 'ASE' (for the estimator network)\n- `cfg.gt.dim_hidden`: typically a narrow-width network (e.g., 4 or 8 hidden dimensions), though `cfg.gt.dim_hidden = 64` is a default.\n- `cfg.gt.n_heads`: 1 (as stated in method and confirmed by warning in `SpExphormer_full_layer.py`)\n- `cfg.prep.exp_deg`: 5 (degree of expander graph)\n- `cfg.prep.exp_algorithm`: 'Hamiltonian' (algorithm for generating expander graphs, other options: 'Random-d', 'Random-d2')\n- `cfg.prep.exp_max_num_iters`: 100 (maximum number of iterations for expander graph generation)\n- `cfg.train.temp_rdc_ratio`: 1.0 (temperature reduction ratio for annealing)\n- `cfg.train.temp_min`: 0.1 (minimum temperature for annealing, method describes 0.05)\n- `cfg.train.temp_wait`: 10 (number of epochs to wait before starting temperature annealing)\n- `cfg.train.save_attention_scores`: False (flag to save attention scores after training the estimator; typically set to True for Phase 1 output)\n\nPhase 2: Sparse Final Network Settings\n- `cfg.gt.layer_type`: 'Spexphormer' (for the final sparse network)\n- `cfg.train.edge_sample_num_neighbors`: [6, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4] (fixed number of edges per attention layer per node, `degℓ`)\n- `cfg.train.spexphormer_sampler`: 'graph_reservoir' (sampling strategy for sparse graphs, other options: 'batch_reservoir', 'graph_uniform', 'graph_max', 'batch_uniform')\n- `cfg.train.resampling_epochs`: 1 (how often to resample edges if `graph_reservoir` sampler is used)\n- The `read_scores` function implies that `ref_dim` (hidden dimension of ASE network) for loading saved attention scores is typically 4 or 8, or 8 for 'ogbn' and 'pokec' datasets."

    "Title": "Degree-Quant: Quantization-Aware Training for Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of making Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) more efficient at inference time by proposing a quantization-aware training method. It identifies unique sources of error when quantizing GNNs, particularly related to straight-through estimator (STE) implementation, node degree, and quantization statistics tracking. The main contribution is Degree-Quant (DQ), an architecture-agnostic and stable quantization-aware training method for GNNs that improves performance over existing baselines. DQ-trained INT8 models achieve accuracy comparable to FP32 models, while INT4 models show up to 26% gains over baselines. The method enables up to 4.7x speedups on CPU with INT8 arithmetic and offers 4-8x reductions in runtime memory usage, and crucially, DQ models generalize to unseen graphs.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology focuses on Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) to simulate quantization during training. DQ employs two main strategies: 1) Stochastic Protection from Quantization to Improve Weight Update Accuracy: This involves stochastically protecting nodes (especially those with high in-degree, which contribute most to gradient error) from quantization by performing message passing, aggregation, and update phases at full precision for masked nodes. A protective node mask is generated per layer using a Bernoulli distribution, with higher probabilities for nodes with larger in-degrees (pmin and pmax hyperparameters for interpolation based on ranking). 2) Percentile Tracking of Quantization Ranges: Instead of min-max or momentum-based tracking, DQ uses percentiles to determine quantization ranges, clipping the top and bottom 0.1% of values to make ranges more representative and reduce rounding error. The method applies uniform quantization to all network elements (weights and activations) and utilizes the Straight-Through Estimator for gradient computation.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was validated on three popular GNN architectures: GCN, GAT, and GIN, conforming to the Message Passing Neural Networks (MPNN) paradigm. Experiments were conducted using 8-bit (INT8) and 4-bit (INT4) quantization for both weights and activations (W8A8, W4A4). Six datasets were used, covering node-level classification (Cora, CiteSeer), graph-level classification (MNIST, CIFAR10 superpixels, REDDIT-BINARY), and graph-level regression (ZINC). Baselines included FP32 models, standard QAT, and noisy QAT (nQAT) which uses stochastic masking of weights. Validation involved extensive hyperparameter tuning using random search and the asynchronous hyperband algorithm. Experiments were run on CPUs (Intel Xeon Gold 6152) and GPUs (GTX 1080Ti) for latency benchmarks. All network elements were quantized, except for the attention mechanism in GAT after softmax, and Batch Normalization layers were left unfolded. For GIN on REDDIT-BINARY, the input layer of the MLP in the first GIN layer was not quantized.",
    "Limitations": "The paper notes that datasets with Gaussian-like node degree distributions are more tolerant of quantization imprecision compared to datasets with tailed distributions, suggesting potential challenges or reduced efficacy for DQ on the latter. A specific compromise was required for GIN models on REDDIT-BINARY, where the input layer of the MLP in the first GIN layer had to remain at full precision due to severe degradation with quantization. Training quantized GNN models can be highly unstable, with some experiments exhibiting large standard deviations (up to 18%), and citation network experiments sometimes failed to converge on GPUs, necessitating CPU execution. The reported speedup results are not considered optimal, as significant time was not devoted to low-level optimizations like cache blocking or kernel fusion.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could involve a more in-depth analysis of how graph topology affects quantization, particularly for datasets with tailed degree distributions. Proposing and studying the impact of a Batch Normalization folding mechanism specifically applicable to GNNs for quantized model deployments is another direction. The authors suggest boosting GNN quantization performance by employing learned step size approaches (e.g., as pioneered by Esser et al., 2020) or robust quantization techniques (e.g., Alizadeh et al., 2020; Shkolnik et al., 2020) to reduce the impact of changing quantization ranges. Furthermore, optimizing the percentile tracking operation using sampling (e.g., 10% or even 1% of data) to accelerate training is proposed for future exploration.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torchfrom torch.autograd.function import InplaceFunctionimport torch.nn as nndef get_qparams(max_val, min_val, num_bits, signed, eps, symmetric):    max_val, min_val = float(max_val), float(min_val)    min_val = min(0.0, min_val)    max_val = max(0.0, max_val)    qmin = -(2.0 ** (num_bits - 1)) if signed else 0.0    qmax = qmin + 2.0 ** num_bits - 1    if max_val == min_val:        scale = 1.0        zero_point = 0    else:        if symmetric:            scale = 2 * max(abs(min_val), max_val) / (qmax - qmin)            zero_point = 0.0 if signed else 128.0        else:            scale = (max_val - min_val) / float(qmax - qmin)            scale = max(scale, eps)            zero_point = qmin - round(min_val / scale)            zero_point = max(qmin, zero_point)            zero_point = min(qmax, zero_point)            zero_point = zero_point    return qmin, qmax, zero_point, scaleclass Quantize(InplaceFunction):    @classmethod    def forward(        cls, ctx, input, max_val, min_val, num_bits, signed, eps, symmetric, ste    ):        output = input.clone()        qmin, qmax, zero_point, scale = get_qparams(            max_val, min_val, num_bits, signed, eps, symmetric        )        ctx.STE = ste        if not ste:            ctx.save_for_backward(input)            ctx.qmin = qmin            ctx.qmax = qmax            ctx.scale =        return output    @staticmethod    def backward(ctx, grad_output):        if ctx.STE:            return grad_output, None, None, None, None, None, None, None        (input,) = ctx.saved_tensors        mask = input.clone()        inv_scale = 1.0 / ctx.scale        mask.mul_(inv_scale).add_(ctx.zp).round_()        grad_input = grad_output.clone()        grad_input[mask.ge(ctx.qmax)] = 0        grad_input[mask.le(ctx.qmin)] = 0        return grad_input, None, None, None, None, None, None, Nonequantize = Quantize.applySAMPLE_CUTOFF = 1000def sample_tensor(prop, x):    if x.numel() < SAMPLE_CUTOFF:        return x    cutoff_prop = SAMPLE_CUTOFF / x.numel()    if cutoff_prop > prop:        prop = cutoff_prop    x = x.view(-1)    probs = torch.tensor([prop], max_val = self.max_val        input = self.sample_fn(input)        current_min = self.min_fn(input)        current_max = self.max_fn(input)        if min_val.numel() == 0 or max_val.numel() == 0:            min_val = current_min            max_val = current_max        else:            if self.momentum_min_max:                min_val = min_val + self.momentum * (current_min - min_val)                max_val = max_val + self.momentum * (current_max - max_val)            else:                min_val = torch.min(current_min, min_val)                max_val = torch.max(current_max, max_val) torch_geometric.data import Batchfrom torch_geometric.utils import degreeclass ProbabilisticHighDegreeMask:    def __init__(self, low_quantise_prob, high_quantise_prob, per_graph=True): degree(graph.edge_index[1], n, dtype=torch.long)        counts = torch.bincount(indegree)        step_size = (self.high_prob - self.low_prob) / n        indegree_ps = counts * step_size (init methods) ...    def forward(self, data):        if hasattr(data, \"prob_mask\") and data.prob_mask is not None:            mask = evaluate_prob_mask(data)        else:            mask = None        x, edge_index, batch = data.x, data.edge_index, data.batch        x = self.conv1(x, edge_index, mask)        for conv in self.convs:            x = conv(x, edge_index, mask)        x = global_mean_pool(x, batch)        x = F.relu(self.lin1(x))        x = F.dropout(x, p=0.5, training=self.training)        x = self.lin2(x)        return F.log_softmax(x, dim=-1)",
    "Experiment Result": "The methodology is applied to the REDDIT-BINARY dataset. Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) is performed with uniform quantization, supporting INT8 and INT4 bit-widths.\n\n**Quantization Settings:**\n- **Bit-width:** Configurable via `--int8` for 8-bit quantization or `--int4` for 4-bit quantization. Default bit-width for `IntegerQuantizer` is 8 for INT8 and 4 for INT4.\n- **Straight-Through Estimator (STE) / Gradient Clipping (GC):** Controlled by mutually exclusive arguments:\n  - `--ste_abs`: STE with absolute min/max range tracking.\n  - `--ste_mom`: STE with momentum-based min/max range tracking.\n  - `--gc_abs`: Gradient clipping with absolute min/max range tracking.\n  - `--gc_mom`: Gradient clipping with momentum-based min/max range tracking.\n  - `--ste_per`: STE with percentile-based range tracking.\n  - `--gc_per`: Gradient clipping with percentile-based range tracking.\n- **Percentile Tracking:** When `--ste_per` or `--gc_per` is used, quantization ranges are determined by clipping values outside a specific percentile. The percentile used is `0.01` (top/bottom 1%) for INT4 and `0.001` (top/bottom 0.1%) for INT8.\n- **Range Tracking Momentum:** When `_mom` flags are used, momentum (default `0.01`) is applied to update min/max ranges. Otherwise, ranges are updated using `torch.min` and `torch.max` (or percentile-based `kthvalue`).\n- **Range Tracking Sampling:** The `sample_prop` argument (default `None`) can be used to sample a subset of tensor elements to determine quantization ranges, particularly when combined with percentile tracking.\n\n**Stochastic Protection (DegreeQuant, DQ) Settings:**\n- **Activation:** Enabled with the `--DQ` flag.\n- **Probability Mask Generation:** The `ProbabilisticHighDegreeMask` generates a protective node mask per layer using a Bernoulli distribution. The probability of a node being protected (i.e., operating at full precision) increases with its in-degree.\n  - `low_quantise_prob` (`--low` argument, default `0.0`): The base probability for nodes with low in-degree.\n  - `high_quantise_prob` (derived from `low_quantise_prob` + `prob_mask_change`):\n    - `prob_mask_change` (`--change` argument, default `0.1`): An increment applied to the base probability, interpolated across nodes based on their in-degree rank, ensuring higher in-degree nodes have higher protection probability.\n  - During training, `MessagePassingMultiQuant` modules apply full precision for masked nodes and quantization for unmasked nodes in message, aggregate, and update phases. Inputs and features in GNN layers also follow this conditional quantization.\n- **Mask Consistency:** Masks can be generated per layer or consistently across layers; per-layer mask generation was explored but not extensively evaluated due to cost.\n\n**Example Experimental Configurations and Results (from `main.py` comments):**\n- **INT8-DQ (DegreeQuant):** Achieved 91.8% accuracy with command: `python main.py --int8 --gc_per --lr 0.005 --DQ --low 0.0 --change 0.1 --wd 0.0002 --epochs 200`\n- **INT8 (Baseline):** Achieved 76.0% accuracy with command: `python main.py --int8 --ste_mom --lr 0.005 --wd 0.0002 --epochs 200`\n- **INT4-DQ (DegreeQuant):** Achieved 81.3% accuracy with command: `python main.py --int4 --gc_per --lr 0.001 --DQ --low 0.1 --change 0.1 --wd 4e-5 --epochs 200`\n- **INT4 (Baseline):** Achieved 54.1% accuracy with command: `python main.py --int4 --ste_mom --lr 0.05 --epochs 200`\n\n**General Training Parameters (default values for GIN model on REDDIT-BINARY):**\n- `epochs`: 200\n- `batch_size`: 128\n- `num_layers`: 5 (for GIN model)\n- `hidden`: 64 (hidden dimensions)\n- `lr`: 0.01 (learning rate)\n- `wd`: 4e-5 (weight decay)\n- `lr_decay_factor`: 0.5\n- `lr_decay_step_size`: 50\n- `dropout`: 0.5 (applied in the final linear layer of GIN)"

    "Title": "$\\rm A^2Q$: Aggregation-Aware Quantization for Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the significant challenge of high latency and memory consumption during Graph Neural Network (GNN) inference by proposing Aggregation-Aware mixed-precision Quantization (A2Q). A2Q automatically learns and assigns an appropriate bitwidth to each node based on the graph's topology, demonstrating that nodes with higher in-degrees benefit from higher bitwidths. This approach achieves up to an 18.6x compression ratio with negligible accuracy degradation compared to FP32 models, and significantly improves accuracy (up to 11.4% on node-level and 9.5% on graph-level tasks) and speedup (up to 2x) over state-of-the-art quantization methods.",
    "Methodology": "A2Q quantizes node features using learnable bitwidths and step sizes for each node. Network weights are quantized to a fixed 4 bits with learnable column-wise step sizes. During training, a memory penalty term is applied to constrain bitwidths and improve compression. To overcome the vanishing gradient problem in semi-supervised tasks due to sparse graph connections, a Local Gradient method is introduced, which uses the quantization error of node features as supervision. For generalizing to unseen graphs in graph-level tasks, a Nearest Neighbor Strategy is developed. This strategy pre-learns a fixed number of quantization parameter groups, and for new nodes, it selects the appropriate group based on the nearest maximum quantization value related to the node's largest absolute feature value. The method leverages a straight-through estimator for non-differentiable quantization functions and is designed for efficient integer operations on a custom hardware accelerator.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The A2Q method was evaluated on three typical GNN models: GCN, GIN, and GAT. Experiments were conducted on eight public datasets, including four node-level semi-supervised tasks (Cora, CiteSeer, PubMed, ogbn-arxiv) and four graph-level tasks (REDDIT-BINARY, MNIST, CIFAR10, ZINC, where ZINC is for regression). Additional evaluations in the appendix included inductive learning tasks (GraphSage on Reddit, Flickr), heterogeneous graphs (ogbn-mag), and link prediction (ogbl-collab). Performance was compared against FP32 models, DQ-INT4, Half-precision, and LPGNAS. Metrics included accuracy (or loss for ZINC), average bitwidths, compression ratio, and inference speedup measured on a dedicated precision-scalable hardware accelerator. Ablation studies investigated the impact of learning-based vs. manual bit assignment, the importance of learning both step size and bitwidth, the effectiveness of Local Gradient, the overhead of the Nearest Neighbor Strategy, GNN depth, skip connections, and different aggregation functions.",
    "Limitations": "The learned bitwidths for GAT models can appear irregular because GAT's aggregated features are described as 'topology-free'. Similarly, in graph-level tasks, node features processed through MLPs and activation functions in deeper layers may lose their original topological information, causing the learned bitwidth to be less correlated with topology. The Nearest Neighbor Strategy's performance is sensitive to the number of quantization parameter groups (`m`), requiring careful selection, as a smaller `m` can reduce accuracy. Models without skip connections are observed to be more sensitive to quantization errors, potentially necessitating higher bitwidths to maintain performance. Existing binary quantization methods, used as baselines, are often restricted to node-level tasks and struggle with complex GNNs, leading to significant accuracy drops.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Potential future research includes developing more sophisticated aggregation-aware quantization strategies for GNN architectures like GAT, where features become less topology-dependent. Further work could explore methods to maintain or re-incorporate topological information in deeper GNN layers to optimize quantization. Research into adaptive or automated mechanisms for determining the optimal number of quantization parameter groups (`m`) for the Nearest Neighbor Strategy would enhance its robustness and ease of use. Additionally, specific quantization schemes could be developed for GNN models without skip connections or other architectures highly sensitive to quantization errors.",
