    "Title": "Are GATs Out of Balance?",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper investigates the optimization and learning dynamics of Graph Attention Networks (GATs), revealing that GATs with standard initialization suffer from a significant portion of parameters failing to change during training, a problem exacerbated in deeper networks. The main contributions include: 1) Deriving a conservation law of GAT gradient flow dynamics, which explains this lack of parameter change. 2) Proposing a novel balanced initialization scheme that allows for more effective gradient propagation, enabling the training of deeper GATs. 3) Demonstrating significant speedup in training and convergence time compared to standard initialization. 4) Establishing an empirical causal relationship between parameter balancedness and trainability. 5) Providing a theoretical stepping stone for studying the learning dynamics of positive homogeneous models with attention mechanisms, including transformers.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology is primarily theoretical and empirical. Theoretically, the authors derive a conservation law for GATs (including variants with shared feature weights and multiple attention heads) using the concept of rescale invariance from traditional deep neural networks and positive homogeneous activation functions (e.g., ReLU). This law quantifies the relationship between gradients and parameters, explaining why standard initializations lead to poor trainability. Empirically, they propose a balanced initialization scheme (Procedure 2.6) where attention parameters are initialized to zero and feature weights are scaled layer-wise to satisfy a derived norm-preservation equality. This scheme is further enhanced by incorporating a Looks-Linear-Orthogonal (LLortho) initialization for feature weights, aiming for initial dynamical isometry.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experiments evaluate GAT models of varying depths (2, 5, 10, 20, 40, 64, 80 layers) and widths on nine common benchmark datasets for semi-supervised node classification: Cora, Citeseer, Pubmed, Actor, Chameleon, Cornell, Squirrel, Texas, and Wisconsin. The models use ReLU activation, weight sharing (unless otherwise specified), and no biases. Training is performed using SGD and Adam optimizers for up to 5000 epochs, with hyperparameter settings adjusted for different depths. Performance is measured by mean test accuracy (%) and epochs to the best model (convergence speedup), with results reported as mean \\u00b195% confidence interval over five runs. The study also analyzes the relative change of network parameters and relative gradient norms across layers. Comparisons are made against standard Xavier initialization, Xavier with zero attention (XavZ), and Lipschitz Normalization [14]. Experiments were run on Nvidia T4 Tensor Core GPU or Nvidia GeForce RTX 3060 Laptop GPU.",
    "Limitations": "The derived conservation law is specifically applicable to the self-attention mechanisms in the original GAT and GATv2 models, and architectural variations like \\u03c9GAT. It requires modifications for other types of self-attention, such as dot-product self-attention found in SuperGAT or Transformers. The theory assumes positively homogeneous activation functions, which means non-homogeneous functions like ELU may negatively impact the proposed orthogonal initialization, although Adam optimizer partially compensates. While the balanced initialization improves trainability, for some heterophilic datasets, standard GNN models (including balanced GATs) do not achieve state-of-the-art performance, as the study focuses on trainability rather than SOTA for such specific challenges. Performance drops at extreme depths (e.g., L=40) might be due to insufficient training epochs rather than inherent trainability issues.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The main theorem serves as a foundation for future research into the learning dynamics of positive homogeneous models that incorporate attention mechanisms, such as Transformers in NLP and Vision Transformers, which often require greater depth. Another direction is to explore how dynamical isometry can be precisely achieved or approximated in general GNN architectures. Furthermore, adapting the conservation law to model different kinds of self-attention, specifically dot-product self-attention mechanisms found in models like SuperGAT and other Transformer-based architectures adapted for graph learning, is an intriguing area. The observation that increased network width can aid generalization performance, even with imbalanced initializations, suggests future work on understanding the role of overparameterization in enabling deeper GNN training.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\n\n\nclass GraphAttentionLayer(nn.Module):\n    \"\"\"\n    Simple GAT layer, similar to https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.10903\n    \"\"\"\n    def __init__(self, in_features, out_features, dropout, alpha, concat=True):\n        super(GraphAttentionLayer, self).__init__()\n        self.dropout = dropout\n        self.in_features = in_features\n        self.out_features = out_features\n        self.alpha = alpha\n        self.concat = concat\n\n        self.W = nn.Parameter(torch.empty(size=(in_features, out_features)))\n        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.W.data, gain=1.414)\n        self.a = nn.Parameter(torch.empty(size=(2*out_features, 1)))\n        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.a.data, gain=1.414)\n\n        self.leakyrelu = nn.LeakyReLU(self.alpha)\n\n    def forward(self, h, adj):\n        Wh = torch.mm(h, self.W) # h.shape: (N, in_features), Wh.shape: (N, out_features)\n        e = self._prepare_attentional_mechanism_input(Wh)\n\n        zero_vec = -9e15*torch.ones_like(e)\n        attention = torch.where(adj > 0, e, zero_vec)\n        attention = F.softmax(attention, dim=1)\n        attention = F.dropout(attention, self.dropout, training=self.training)\n        h_prime = torch.matmul(attention, Wh)\n\n        if self.concat:\n            return F.elu(h_prime)\n        else:\n            return h_prime\n\n    def _prepare_attentional_mechanism_input(self, Wh):\n        # Wh.shape (N, out_feature)\n        # self.a.shape (2 * out_feature, 1)\n        # Wh1&2.shape (N, 1)\n        # e.shape (N, N)\n        Wh1 = torch.matmul(Wh, self.a[:self.out_features, :])\n        Wh2 = torch.matmul(Wh, self.a[self.out_features:, :])\n        # broadcast add\n        e = Wh1 + Wh2.T\n        return self.leakyrelu(e)\n\n    def __repr__(self):\n        return self.__class__.__name__ + ' (' + str(self.in_features) + ' -> ' + str(self.out_features) + ')'\n\n\nclass SpecialSpmmFunction(torch.autograd.Function):\n    \"\"\"Special function for only sparse region backpropataion layer.\"\"\"\n    @staticmethod\n    def forward(ctx, indices, values, shape, b):\n        assert indices.requires_grad == False\n        a = torch.sparse_coo_tensor(indices, values, shape)\n        ctx.save_for_backward(a, b)\n        ctx.N = shape[0]\n        return torch.matmul(a, b)\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def backward(ctx, grad_output):\n        a, b = ctx.saved_tensors\n        grad_values = grad_b = None\n        if ctx.needs_input_grad[1]:\n            grad_a_dense = grad_output.matmul(b.t())\n            edge_idx = a._indices()[0, :] * ctx.N + a._indices()[1, :]\n            grad_values = grad_a_dense.view(-1)[edge_idx]\n        if ctx.needs_input_grad[3]:\n            grad_b = a.t().matmul(grad_output)\n        return None, grad_values, None, grad_b\n\n\nclass SpecialSpmm(nn.Module):\n    def forward(self, indices, values, shape, b):\n        return SpecialSpmmFunction.apply(indices, values, shape, b)\n\n    \nclass SpGraphAttentionLayer(nn.Module):\n    \"\"\"\n    Sparse version GAT layer, similar to https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.10903\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, in_features, out_features, dropout, alpha, concat=True):\n        super(SpGraphAttentionLayer, self).__init__()\n        self.in_features = in_features\n        self.out_features = out_features\n        self.alpha = alpha\n        self.concat = concat\n\n        self.W = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(size=(in_features, out_features)))\n        nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.W.data, gain=1.414)\n                \n        self.a = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(size=(1, 2*out_features)))\n        nn.init.xavier_normal_(self.a.data, gain=1.414)\n\n        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)\n        self.leakyrelu = nn.LeakyReLU(self.alpha)\n        self.special_spmm = SpecialSpmm()\n\n    def forward(self, input, adj):\n        dv = 'cuda' if input.is_cuda else 'cpu'\n\n        N = input.size()[0]\n        edge = adj.nonzero().t()\n\n        h = torch.mm(input, self.W)\n        # h: N x out\n        assert not torch.isnan(h).any()\n\n        # Self-attention on the nodes - Shared attention mechanism\n        edge_h = torch.cat((h[edge[0, :], :], h[edge[1, :], :]), dim=1).t()\n        # edge: 2*D x E\n\n        edge_e = torch.exp(-self.leakyrelu(self.a.mm(edge_h).squeeze()))\n        assert not torch.isnan(edge_e).any()\n        # edge_e: E\n\n        e_rowsum = self.special_spmm(edge, edge_e, torch.Size([N, N]), torch.ones(size=(N,1), device=dv))\n        # e_rowsum: N x 1\n\n        edge_e = self.dropout(edge_e)\n        # edge_e: E\n\n        h_prime = self.special_spmm(edge, edge_e, torch.Size([N, N]), h)\n        assert not torch.isnan(h_prime).any()\n        # h_prime: N x out\n        \n        h_prime = h_prime.div(e_rowsum)\n        # h_prime: N x out\n        assert not torch.isnan(h_prime).any()\n\n        if self.concat:\n            # if this layer is not last layer,\n            return F.elu(h_prime)\n        else:\n            # if this layer is last layer,\n            return h_prime\n\n    def __repr__(self):\n        return self.__class__.__name__ + ' (' + str(self.in_features) + ' -> ' + str(self.out_features) + ')'\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom layers import GraphAttentionLayer, SpGraphAttentionLayer\n\n\nclass GAT(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, nfeat, nhid, nclass, dropout, alpha, nheads):\n        \"\"\"Dense version of GAT.\"\"\"\n        super(GAT, self).__init__()\n        self.dropout = dropout\n\n        self.attentions = [GraphAttentionLayer(nfeat, nhid, dropout=dropout, alpha=alpha, concat=True) for _ in range(nheads)]\n        for i, attention in enumerate(self.attentions):\n            self.add_module('attention_{}'.format(i), attention)\n\n        self.out_att = GraphAttentionLayer(nhid * nheads, nclass, dropout=dropout, alpha=alpha, concat=False)\n\n    def forward(self, x, adj):\n        x = F.dropout(x, self.dropout, training=self.training)\n        x = torch.cat([att(x, adj) for att in self.attentions], dim=1)\n        x = F.dropout(x, self.dropout, training=self.training)\n        x = F.elu(self.out_att(x, adj))\n        return F.log_softmax(x, dim=1)\n\n\nclass SpGAT(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, nfeat, nhid, nclass, dropout, alpha, nheads):\n        \"\"\"Sparse version of GAT.\"\"\"\n        super(SpGAT, self).__init__()\n        self.dropout = dropout\n\n        self.attentions = [SpGraphAttentionLayer(nfeat, \n                                                 nhid, \n                                                 dropout=dropout, \n                                                 alpha=alpha, \n                                                 concat=True) for _ in range(nheads)]\n        for i, attention in enumerate(self.attentions):\n            self.add_module('attention_{}'.format(i), attention)\n\n        self.out_att = SpGraphAttentionLayer(nhid * nheads, \n                                             nclass, \n                                             dropout=dropout, \n                                             alpha=alpha, \n                                             concat=False)\n\n    def forward(self, x, adj):\n        x = F.dropout(x, self.dropout, training=self.training)\n        x = torch.cat([att(x, adj) for att in self.attentions], dim=1)\n        x = F.dropout(x, self.dropout, training=self.training)\n        x = F.elu(self.out_att(x, adj))\n        return F.log_softmax(x, dim=1)\n",
    "Experiment Result": "The provided repository content implements standard GAT (Graph Attention Network) and SpGAT (Sparse GAT) models, which are the subject of the theoretical analysis in the method.     "Title": "Quantizable Transformers: Removing Outliers by Helping Attention Heads Do Nothing",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper identifies that strong outliers in Transformer model activations, which hinder efficient quantization, are caused by attention heads attempting to learn 'no-op' or partial updates to the residual connection, leading to large softmax inputs. To address this, the authors propose two novel modifications to the attention mechanism: Clipped Softmax and Gated Attention. These methods enable models to learn significantly smaller outliers while maintaining or even improving floating-point task performance, thereby allowing for full INT8 quantization of activations without additional effort. The effectiveness is demonstrated across language models (BERT, OPT) and vision transformers (ViT).",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves modifying the attention mechanism to allow for exact zero outputs without generating outliers. The first method, Clipped Softmax, replaces the standard softmax function with a clipped variant: `clipped_softmax(x; ζ, γ) := clip ((ζ − γ) · softmax(x) + γ, 0, 1)`. This stretches the softmax output and clips it back to [0, 1], allowing for exact zeros (when γ < 0) or ones (when ζ > 1) with finite input ranges, and stopping gradient propagation for clipped values. The second method, Gated Attention, introduces an explicit conditional gating mechanism: `Gated_attention(x) := sigmoid (G(x)) ⊙ softmax (Q(x)K(x)T/√dhead) V(x)`. A lightweight neural network `G(x)`, typically a single linear layer per head, learns token-wise gating probabilities to selectively nullify or keep updates to hidden representations. Both modifications are applied during pre-training.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The methods were evaluated on various models and tasks. Language models include BERT-base-uncased (109M parameters) pre-trained with masked language modeling (MLM) on BookCorpus and English Wikipedia, and fine-tuned on MNLI. OPT-125M, OPT-350M, and OPT-1.3B variants were pre-trained with causal language modeling (CLM) on BookCorpus and English Wikipedia. Vision Transformers (ViT-S/16, 22M parameters) were trained on ImageNet-1K. Evaluation metrics included MLM perplexity for BERT, CLM perplexity for OPT, and Top-1 accuracy for ViT. Outlier assessment used maximum infinity norm (∥x∥∞) and kurtosis of attention layer outputs. For quantization, 8-bit Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) was applied, using uniform affine quantization (symmetric weights, asymmetric activations) with static range estimation (running min-max, sometimes 99.999% percentiles for OPT). All weights and activations were quantized, except the final linear layer for BERT and OPT. Experiments also explored lower bitwidths (W6A8, W4A8, W6A6). The implementation used PyTorch, HuggingFace libraries, and PyTorch Image models library, with FP16 mixed-precision.",
    "Limitations": "The scalability of the methods was studied up to 1.3B parameter models, but not for 'very large transformers that are trained for way longer.' The authors cautiously state that observed small improvements in FP16/FP32 performance may not generalize to more architectures or larger models consistently. Each proposed method introduces a hyperparameter (e.g., γ for clipped softmax, bias initialization for gated attention), which, while shown to be robust, is 'never optimal.' A specific limitation noted is the failure of clipped softmax when applied to OPT, for which no explanation is provided, leaving it for future work.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include further investigation into the identified 'no-op' behavior, particularly in other neural network architectures like convolutional networks, and its potential role in network regularization and generalization. The authors also suggest exploring the combination of their proposed methods with 'more advanced quantization and weight compression methods' (e.g., from references [18, 35, 36, 45, 63, 67]) to achieve even better low-bit quantization performance. Additionally, understanding why clipped softmax failed for OPT is highlighted as an area for future work. Finally, generalizing the observed floating-point performance improvements to a wider range of architectures and larger models remains an open question.",

    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Gated Linear Attention Transformers with Hardware-Efficient Training",
    "Main Contributions": "The research introduces a hardware-efficient algorithm for linear attention, FLASH LINEAR ATTENTION, which outperforms FLASH ATTENTION-2. This algorithm is then generalized to Gated Linear Attention (GLA) with data-dependent gates, resulting in the GLA Transformer. This model achieves competitive performance against LLaMA-architecture Transformers and recent linear-time models like RetNet and Mamba on language modeling benchmarks. A key finding is its exceptional effectiveness at length generalization, allowing models trained on 2K sequences to generalize to over 20K with minimal perplexity degradation. Additionally, the GLA Transformer demonstrates higher training throughput than similarly sized Mamba models.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves developing an I/O-aware, hardware-efficient algorithm for linear attention, named FLASH LINEAR ATTENTION, based on a chunkwise parallel form. It employs tiling to load tensor blocks, reusing them on-chip to reduce HBM I/O. Two versions are presented: a memory-efficient non-materialization version (parallelizing over batch size) and a materialization version (enabling sequence-level parallelism via recomputation). This algorithm is extended to Gated Linear Attention (GLA) by incorporating a data-dependent 2D forget gate (Gt = α⊤ t 1) into the linear recurrence (St = Gt ⊙ St-1 + k⊤ t vt). For hardware-efficient GLA, secondary-level chunking is used where inter-sub-chunk computations leverage half-precision matmuls for tensor cores, while intra-sub-chunk parts use full-precision log-space calculations for stability. A memory-efficient closed-form formula for dlogαt gradients is also derived to avoid large hidden state materialization. The GLA Transformer architecture interleaves multi-head GLA layers with SwiGLU feed-forward networks, applying LayerNorm per head and using output gating on concatenated head outputs. A low-rank parameterization (αt =σ((xtW1 αW2 α +bα))) is used for parameter-efficiency.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments focused on language modeling using the SlimPajama dataset (a 100B token subset of the 627B original) and the Mistral tokenizer. Models were trained at two scales: 340M parameters (on 15B tokens) and 1.3B parameters (on 100B tokens) using AdamW optimizer with a cosine learning rate schedule, weight decay, and gradient clipping. Baselines included Transformer++ (LLaMA architecture with RoPE, SWiGLU, RMSNorm), RetNet, and Mamba. Evaluation involved perplexity on Wikitext and LAMBADA, and accuracy on various zero-shot downstream tasks covering common-sense reasoning and question-answering (PIQA, HellaSwag, WinoGrande, ARC-easy/challenge, COPA, SciQA, OpenbookQA, BoolQ). Recall-intensive tasks included a synthetic MQAR task and real-world tasks like FDA, SWDE, and SQUAD. Length extrapolation capabilities were assessed on SlimPajama and PG19 test sets by training models on 2K, 8K, and 24K contexts (the latter via truncated BPTT). Training throughput and memory usage were benchmarked on a single H100 GPU.",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitation is the inability to conduct larger-scale experiments (e.g., > 7B models or datasets) due to restricted compute resources, leaving the scalability of GLA at much larger scales empirically unconfirmed, although it is anticipated to be favorable compared to Mamba. The paper also acknowledges that it did not explore integrating other types of feature maps (e.g., scaled element-wise exponential, higher-order polynomial kernels) into GLA. Furthermore, the research did not investigate how these new architectures might affect known issues such as biased and harmful outputs of language models.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include performing larger-scale experiments to empirically confirm the training efficiency and performance benefits of GLA when scaled to models larger than 7B parameters. Exploring the application of GLA to other modalities, particularly those with long-range dependencies like audio or video, is also suggested. Investigating the integration of alternative feature maps (e.g., scaled element-wise exponential or higher-order polynomial kernels) into the GLA framework could lead to further improvements. Leveraging GLA's inherent compatibility with tensor parallelism is a direction for enhancing efficiency at larger scales. Lastly, a crucial area for future work is to study the impact of GLA architectures on known issues of large language models, such as biased or harmful outputs.",

    "Title": "GATE: How to Keep Out Intrusive Neighbors",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper identifies and experimentally demonstrates a structural limitation of Graph Attention Networks (GATs): their inability to switch off task-irrelevant neighborhood aggregation, which leads to over-smoothing and hinders performance on certain tasks. To address this, the authors propose GATE (GAT Extension), which modifies the GAT architecture to flexibly weight the importance of a node's own features versus its neighbors' features. GATE alleviates over-smoothing, benefits from increased model depth by utilizing layers for non-linear feature transformations, and often outperforms GATs on real-world heterophilic datasets. It also offers interpretable learned self-attention coefficients. Furthermore, the paper constructs a synthetic test bed to analyze a model's ability to utilize appropriate neighborhood aggregation, which could be of independent interest.",
    "Methodology": "GATE is an extension of the GAT architecture (specifically GATv2). While GAT layers use attention coefficients alpha_uv to aggregate neighbor features, GATE modifies the computation of the raw attention score `e_uv` (from Eq. 3 to Eq. 4). This modification allows GATE to learn separate attention parameters (`a_s` and `a_t`) for the node's own contribution and the neighborhood's contribution, enabling it to switch neighborhood aggregation on and off as needed. The theoretical basis for this improvement is rooted in a modified conservation law of GAT gradient flow dynamics (Theorem 4.3), which indicates that GATE can switch off neighborhood aggregation in a well-trainable parameter regime. The model uses ReLU as the non-linear activation function in its attention mechanism for interpretability of `a_s` and `a_t` signs.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The validation of GATE's capabilities was conducted on both synthetic and real-world graphs. The synthetic test bed includes two types of node classification problems: self-sufficient learning (label-relevant information only in node's own features) and neighbor-dependent learning (label-relevant information only in k-hop neighbors' features), using Erdős–Rényi graphs and the Cora graph structure. Real-world evaluations used five heterophilic benchmark datasets (roman-empire, amazon-ratings, questions, minesweeper, tolokers) and three OGB datasets (OGB-arxiv, OGB-products, OGB-mag), along with five smaller-scale datasets (Cora, Citeseer, Actor, Texas, Wisconsin) with varying homophily levels. Performance was measured by test accuracy or AUC-ROC. GATE was compared against GAT, MLP, MLP+GAT, FAGCN, GAT-sep, GT, GT-sep, GCN, SAGE, H2GCN, CPGNN, GPR-GNN, FSGNN, GloGNN, GBK-GNN, JacobiConv, ResNet, ResNet+SGC, ResNet+adj, and ωGAT. Models were trained using the Adam optimizer without weight decay or dropout, with varying learning rates per dataset.",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitation identified and addressed is the structural inability of standard GATs to effectively switch off task-irrelevant neighborhood aggregation, leading to over-smoothing and hindering performance, especially with increased model depth. For GATE, the paper notes that for challenging neighbor-dependent tasks, it may not achieve perfect 100% test accuracy, possibly due to data points being close to a fuzzy decision boundary. Additionally, the notion of 'over-smoothing' itself is acknowledged as task-dependent and difficult to define optimally. For smaller real-world datasets, deeper models are prone to overfitting without additional elements like skip connections or regularization, which were intentionally excluded in some comparative experiments to isolate architectural effects.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper suggests that GATE is a suitable candidate to answer highly debated questions related to the importance of a given graph structure for standard tasks. It also proposes that the capacity of FAGCN to learn negative associations with neighboring nodes is complementary to GATE, implying a potential direction for combining these approaches. Furthermore, the authors recommend investigating and deriving conservation laws inherent to other GNN architectures like FAGCN and GraphSAGE to understand how they govern parameter behavior and model expressiveness.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "$\\rm A^2Q$: Aggregation-Aware Quantization for Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of high latency and memory consumption in Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) inference, proposing Aggregation-Aware mixed-precision Quantization (A2Q). A2Q automatically learns and assigns an appropriate bitwidth to each node based on the graph's topology, significantly reducing model complexity without substantial accuracy degradation. Key contributions include the A2Q method itself, a Local Gradient method to mitigate vanishing gradients in semi-supervised tasks, and a Nearest Neighbor Strategy for generalizing to unseen graphs. The method achieves up to 18.6x compression with negligible accuracy drop compared to FP32 models and up to 11.4% accuracy improvement over state-of-the-art quantization with up to 2x speedup on a dedicated hardware accelerator.",
    "Methodology": "The A2Q method quantizes node features with adaptive learnable step sizes (αi) and bitwidths (bi) for each node, guided by a penalty on memory size. Weights are uniformly quantized to 4-bits with learnable column-wise step sizes (βi). Matrix multiplications are reformulated for integer operations. To address the vanishing gradient problem in semi-supervised tasks due to sparse connections and few labeled nodes, a Local Gradient method is introduced, using the L1 quantization error as supervision for node feature parameter training. For generalization to unseen graphs with varying node numbers (graph-level tasks), the Nearest Neighbor Strategy is proposed: it learns 'm' groups of quantization parameters and assigns a group to each new node by finding the closest maximum quantization value based on the node's largest absolute feature value. The method relies on a straight-through estimator for backpropagation through non-differentiable quantization functions.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on GCN, GIN, and GAT models. Node-level tasks used Cora, CiteSeer, PubMed, and ogbn-arxiv datasets, with additional tests on ogbn-mag (heterogeneous), ogbl-collab (link prediction), and Flickr (inductive) using GraphSage. Graph-level tasks included REDDIT-BINARY, MNIST, CIFAR10, and ZINC datasets. Comparisons were made against FP32 baselines, DQ-INT4, Half-precision, LPGNAS, and Binary Quantization methods. Model architectures were kept consistent with baselines for fair comparison. Experiments used Adam optimizer, standard data splits, and were averaged over multiple runs (100 for some node-level, 10 for others, 10-fold cross-validation for REDDIT-BINARY). Speedup evaluations were performed on a custom precision-scalable hardware accelerator, with energy efficiency compared against a 2080Ti GPU.",
    "Limitations": "While the paper's proposed methods address several challenges, an implicit limitation is that the reported speedups are primarily achieved on a custom precision-scalable hardware accelerator, as 'today's CPUs and GPUs can not support mixed-precision operations well.' This suggests that direct deployment on general-purpose hardware might not yield the same inference speed benefits without further software optimization or specialized libraries. Additionally, for certain GNN architectures like GAT, the learned bitwidths for nodes were observed to be 'irregular' and not directly correlated with topology, though the method still performed well. For datasets like ZINC with similar node topology structures, the benefits of learning different bitwidths per node might be reduced. Deeper GNNs still suffer from the 'over-smooth phenomenon,' although the method learns higher bitwidths for deeper layers to mitigate quantization error.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Not mentioned",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Learnable Graph Convolutional Attention Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The research addresses the problem that existing Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), such as Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) and Graph Attention Networks (GATs), exhibit data-dependent performance, necessitating computationally expensive cross-validation for architecture selection. The main contributions are: 1. The introduction of the Graph Convolutional Attention Layer (CAT), which integrates convolution into attention score computation, aiming to combine the strengths of GCNs and GATs. 2. The development of the Learnable Graph Convolutional Attention Network (L-CAT), a novel GNN architecture that automatically interpolates between GCN, GAT, and CAT within each layer using two learnable scalar parameters. L-CAT consistently outperforms competing methods across a wide range of datasets, demonstrating increased robustness to input noise and network initialization, thereby significantly reducing the need for manual architectural cross-validation.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology introduces two key components. First, the Graph Convolutional Attention Layer (CAT) computes attention scores by utilizing convolved features (averaged neighborhood features) as inputs to its score function. This is formalized as Ψ(hi,hj) = α(W˜hi,W˜hj) where ˜hi = 1/|N∗i| ∑ℓ∈N∗i hℓ. Second, the Learnable Graph Convolutional Attention Network (L-CAT) builds upon this by introducing a learnable interpolation mechanism. L-CAT's attention score is defined as Ψ(hi,hj) = λ1 ·α(W˜hi,W˜hj) where ˜hi = hi + λ2 ∑ℓ∈Ni hℓ / (1 + λ2|Ni|). Two scalar parameters, λ1 and λ2 (constrained to [0,1] via a sigmoid function), are learned per layer, enabling L-CAT to dynamically interpolate between a GCN (λ1=0), GAT (λ1=1, λ2=0), and CAT (λ1=1, λ2=1) operation. The theoretical analysis employs a contextual stochastic block model (CSBM) to compare the different GNN architectures.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes evaluations on synthetic data and real-world datasets. Synthetic data experiments use a contextual stochastic block model (CSBM) with 10,000 nodes, p=0.5, σ=0.1, and d=n/(5 log2(n)) dimensions to validate theoretical findings, varying noise levels (q) and mean distances (∥µ∥), with results averaged over 50 runs. Real-world node classification tasks are conducted on six datasets (Amazon Computers, Amazon Photo, GitHub, FacebookPagePage, Coauthor Physics, TwitchEN, and additional datasets like CoauthorCS, DBLP, PubMed, Cora, CiteSeer from the appendix). Models use four GNN layers with 32 hidden units, and hyperparameters are cross-validated using GraphGym, with results averaged over ten runs. Open Graph Benchmark (OGB) datasets (ogbn-arxiv, ogbn-products, ogbn-mag, ogbn-proteins) are used for larger-scale and robustness assessments. OGB experiments adopt setups from previous work (Brody et al., 2022) or OGB's example code, with models using 1 or 8 attention heads, and results averaged over five runs. Robustness studies include adding Gaussian noise to node features and Bernoulli noise to edges (on ogbn-arxiv) and testing different network initializations (Uniform Glorot vs. Normal Glorot on ogbn-proteins). Performance metrics are node classification accuracy (%) and AUC-ROC (%).",
    "Limitations": "The study reveals that the optimal performance of GCN, GAT, and CAT is highly dependent on the nature of the data (graph structure and features), meaning no single architecture is universally superior. Theoretically, convolutions in CAT are not always beneficial, and can even reduce separability in certain noisy graph regimes. Empirically, GCN remains a strong baseline in many real-world scenarios, indicating that architectural complexity does not guarantee superior performance. Baseline attention models (GAT, GATv2) are sensitive to feature noise and can perform poorly with certain network initializations. Conversely, GCNs are more susceptible to edge noise. The theoretical analysis relies on specific assumptions about graph structure and feature distributions (e.g., degree concentration in CSBM).",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that the learnable interpolation mechanism introduced by L-CAT can motivate new and exciting research in GNNs. Future work should focus on applying L-CAT in various real-world applications to explore the practical benefits of combining different GNN layer types within a single model, without the significant overhead of architectural cross-validation. This also implies further investigation into extending the L-CAT framework to a broader array of complex GNN architectures beyond the demonstrated PNA and GCNII examples.",
    "Experiment Code": "import math\nfrom typing import Optional, Union, Tuple\n\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nfrom torch import Tensor\nfrom torch_geometric.nn.conv import MessagePassing\nfrom torch_geometric.nn.inits import constant\nfrom torch_geometric.typing import Adj, OptTensor, PairTensor\nfrom torch_geometric.utils import add_self_loops, remove_self_loops, softmax\nfrom torch_sparse import set_diag, SparseTensor\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom torch_geometric.nn.dense.linear import Linear, Parameter\n\nclass GeneralGATLayer(MessagePassing):\n    _alpha: OptTensor\n\n    def __init__(\n            self,\n            in_channels: Union[int, Tuple[int, int]],\n            out_channels: int,\n            negative_slope: float = 0.2,\n            add_self_loops: bool = True,\n            heads: int = 1,\n            bias: bool = True,\n            mode: str = 'gcn',\n            aggr: str = 'mean',\n            **kwargs,\n    ):\n        super().__init__(aggr=aggr, node_dim=0, **kwargs)\n        assert mode in ['gcn', 'gat', 'cat', 'lcat', 'gcngat', 'gatcat']\n\n        self.in_channels = in_channels\n        self.out_channels = out_channels\n        self.heads = heads\n        self.negative_slope = negative_slope\n        self.add_self_loops = add_self_loops\n\n        self.convolve = mode in ['gcn', 'cat', 'lcat', 'gatcat']\n\n        self.learn_l1 = mode in ['lcat', 'gcngat']\n        self.learn_l2 = mode in ['lcat', 'gatcat']\n\n        self.mode = mode\n        self.lmbda_ = None\n        self.lmbda2_ = None\n\n        if self.learn_l1:\n            self.lmbda_ = nn.Parameter(torch.ones([]) * 0, requires_grad=True)\n        if self.learn_l2:\n            self.lmbda2_ = nn.Parameter(torch.ones([]) * 0, requires_grad=True)\n\n        self.bias = 0.\n        if bias:\n            self.bias = nn.Parameter(torch.Tensor(out_channels * self.heads))\n\n    @property\n    def lmbda(self):  # The one that controls GCN-->GATConv (λ1 in paper)\n        if self.learn_l1:\n            return torch.sigmoid(10 ** self.lmbda_ - 6)\n        else:\n            if self.mode in ['gcn']:\n                return 0.\n            elif self.mode in ['gat', 'cat', 'gatcat']:\n                return 1.0\n            else:\n                raise NotImplementedError\n\n    @property\n    def lmbda2(self):  # The one that controls GATConv-->GAT (λ2 in paper)\n        if self.learn_l2:\n            return torch.sigmoid(10 ** (2.2 - self.lmbda2_) - 6)\n        else:\n            if self.mode in ['gcn', 'gat', 'gcngat']:\n                return 0.0\n            elif self.mode in ['cat']:\n                return 1.\n            else:\n                raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def reset_parameters(self):\n        constant(self.lmbda_, math.log10(6))\n        constant(self.lmbda2_, 2.2 - math.log10(6))\n        constant(self.bias, 0.)\n\n    def get_x_r(self, x):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def get_x_l(self, x):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def get_x_v(self, x):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def get_x_agg(self, x_l, x_r, edge_index, edge_weight):\n        if isinstance(edge_index, Tensor):\n            edge_index_no_neigh, edge_weight_no_neigh = remove_self_loops(edge_index, edge_weight)\n        elif isinstance(edge_index, SparseTensor):\n            edge_index_no_neigh = set_diag(edge_index, 0)\n            edge_weight_no_neigh = None\n        else:\n            raise NotImplementedError\n\n        aggr = self.aggr\n        self.aggr = 'add'\n        # x_lr represents the current node's transformed features (concat of x_l and x_r) for attention\n        x_lr = torch.cat((x_l, x_r), dim=1)\n        # Sum of neighbor features (∑ℓ∈Ni hℓ)\n        x_neig_sum = self.propagate(edge_index_no_neigh, x=(x_lr, x_lr), size=None, convolve=True,\n                                    edge_weight=edge_weight_no_neigh)\n        self.aggr = aggr\n\n        # Core interpolation for ~hi: λ1_code * (hi + λ2_code * ∑ℓ∈Ni hℓ)\n        x_agg = self.lmbda * (x_lr + self.lmbda2 * x_neig_sum)\n\n        # Divide by (1 + λ2_code * |Ni|)\n        i, j = (1, 0) if self.flow == 'source_to_target' else (0, 1)\n        if isinstance(edge_index, Tensor):\n            counts = x_agg.new_zeros((x_agg.size(0),))\n            a, b = edge_index_no_neigh[i].unique(return_counts=True)\n            counts = counts.scatter_add(0, a, b.float())\n        elif isinstance(edge_index, SparseTensor):\n            counts = edge_index_no_neigh.sum(dim=j)\n\n        x_agg = x_agg / (1 + self.lmbda2 * counts.unsqueeze(-1).unsqueeze(-1))\n\n        return x_agg\n\n    def merge_heads(self, x):\n        return x.flatten(start_dim=-2)\n\n    def compute_score(self, x_i, x_j, index, ptr, size_i):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def fix_parameters(self, partial=False):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def forward(self, x: Union[Tensor, PairTensor], edge_index: Adj, size_target: int = None,\n                edge_weight: OptTensor = None, return_attention_info: bool = False):\n\n        assert isinstance(x, Tensor) and x.dim() == 2\n\n        # Apply linear layers before potential convolution\n        x_l = self.get_x_l(x).view(-1, self.heads, self.out_channels)\n        x_r = self.get_x_r(x).view(-1, self.heads, self.out_channels)\n        x_v = self.get_x_v(x).view(-1, self.heads, self.out_channels)\n\n        num_nodes = x.size(0)\n        if size_target is not None:\n            num_nodes = min(num_nodes, size_target)\n\n        if self.convolve: # This branch is active for CAT and L-CAT\n            x_agg = self.get_x_agg(x_l=x_l, x_r=x_r, edge_index=edge_index, edge_weight=edge_weight)\n            # x_l and x_r are now derived from the convolved/interpolated features (~hi)\n            x_l, x_r = x_agg[:, :self.heads], x_agg[:, self.heads:]\n\n        x_r = x_r[:num_nodes]\n\n        if self.add_self_loops:\n            if isinstance(edge_index, Tensor):\n                edge_index, edge_weight = remove_self_loops(edge_index, edge_weight)\n                edge_index, edge_weight = add_self_loops(edge_index, edge_weight, num_nodes=num_nodes)\n            elif isinstance(edge_index, SparseTensor):\n                edge_index = set_diag(edge_index, 1.)\n\n        i, j = (1, 0) if self.flow == 'source_to_target' else (0, 1)\n        x_lr = [x_l, x_r]\n        x_lr[j] = torch.cat((x_lr[j], x_v), dim=-1)\n\n        out = self.propagate(edge_index, x=x_lr, edge_weight=edge_weight, size=None)\n\n        alpha = self._alpha\n        del self._alpha\n\n        score = self._score\n        del self._score\n\n        out = self.update_fn(x_agg=out, x_i=x_v)\n\n        if return_attention_info:\n            assert alpha is not None\n            return out, (edge_index, alpha), score\n        else:\n            return out\n\n    def update_fn(self, x_agg, x_i):\n        return self.merge_heads(x_agg) + self.bias\n\n    def message(self, x_j: Tensor,\n                x_i: Tensor, index: Tensor,\n                ptr: OptTensor,\n                size_i: Optional[int],\n                edge_weight: OptTensor,\n                convolve=False) -> Tensor:\n        if convolve:\n            return x_j\n\n        s = x_i.size(-1)\n        x_j, x_v = x_j[..., :s], x_j[..., s:]\n\n        score = self.compute_score(x_i, x_j, index, ptr, size_i)\n        self._alpha = softmax(score, index, ptr, size_i)\n        self._score = score\n\n        num_neighbors = softmax(torch.ones_like(score), index, ptr, size_i).reciprocal()\n\n        edge_weight = 1. if edge_weight is None else edge_weight.view(-1, 1, 1)\n        return x_v * (self._alpha * num_neighbors) * edge_weight\n\n    def __repr__(self) -> str:\n        return (f'{self.__class__.__name__}({self.in_channels}, '\n                f'{self.out_channels}))'\n\n\nclass GATv1Layer(GeneralGATLayer):\n    def __init__(\n            self,\n            in_channels: Union[int, Tuple[int, int]],\n            out_channels: int,\n            negative_slope: float = 0.2,\n            add_self_loops: bool = True,\n            heads: int = 1,\n            bias: bool = True,\n            mode: str = 'gcn',\n            share_weights_score: bool = False,\n            share_weights_value: bool = False,\n            aggr: str = 'mean',\n            **kwargs,\n    ):\n        super().__init__(in_channels,\n                         out_channels,\n                         negative_slope,\n                         add_self_loops,\n                         heads, bias, mode, aggr,\n                         **kwargs)\n\n        self.lin_l = Linear(in_channels, out_channels * self.heads, bias=bias, weight_initializer='glorot')\n        if share_weights_score:\n            self.lin_r = self.lin_l\n        else:\n            self.lin_r = Linear(in_channels, out_channels * self.heads, bias=bias, weight_initializer='glorot')\n\n        if share_weights_value:\n            self.lin_v = self.lin_l if self.flow == 'source_to_target' else self.lin_r\n        else:\n            self.lin_v = Linear(in_channels, out_channels * self.heads, bias=bias, weight_initializer='glorot')\n\n        self.att_r = Parameter(torch.Tensor(1, self.heads, out_channels))\n        self.att_l = Parameter(torch.Tensor(1, self.heads, out_channels))\n\n        self.reset_parameters()\n\n    def reset_parameters(self):\n        super().reset_parameters()\n        constant(self.att_r, 1.)\n        constant(self.att_l, 1.)\n        self.lin_l.reset_parameters()\n        self.lin_r.reset_parameters()\n        self.lin_v.reset_parameters()\n\n    def get_x_r(self, x):\n        return self.lin_r(x).view(-1, self.heads, self.out_channels) * self.att_r\n\n    def get_x_l(self, x):\n        return self.lin_l(x).view(-1, self.heads, self.out_channels) * self.att_l\n\n    def get_x_v(self, x):\n        return self.lin_v(x)\n\n    def compute_score(self, x_i, x_j, index, ptr, size_i):\n        return F.leaky_relu(torch.sum(x_i + x_j, dim=-1, keepdim=True), self.negative_slope)\n\n\nclass GATv2Layer(GeneralGATLayer):\n    def __init__(\n            self,\n            in_channels: Union[int, Tuple[int, int]],\n            out_channels: int,\n            negative_slope: float = 0.2,\n            add_self_loops: bool = True,\n            heads: int = 1,\n            bias: bool = True,\n            mode: str = 'gcn',\n            share_weights_score: bool = False,\n            share_weights_value: bool = False,\n            aggr: str = 'mean',\n            **kwargs,\n    ):\n        super().__init__(in_channels,\n                         out_channels,\n                         negative_slope,\n                         add_self_loops,\n                         heads,\n                         bias,\n                         mode,\n                         aggr,\n                         **kwargs)\n\n        self.lin_l = Linear(in_channels, out_channels * self.heads, bias=bias, weight_initializer='glorot')\n        if share_weights_score:\n            self.lin_r = self.lin_l\n        else:\n            self.lin_r = Linear(in_channels, out_channels * self.heads, bias=bias, weight_initializer='glorot')\n\n        if share_weights_value:\n            self.lin_v = self.lin_l if self.flow == 'source_to_target' else self.lin_r\n        else:\n            self.lin_v = Linear(in_channels, out_channels * self.heads, bias=bias, weight_initializer='glorot')\n\n        self.att = Parameter(torch.Tensor(1, self.heads, out_channels))\n\n        self.reset_parameters()\n\n    def reset_parameters(self):\n        super().reset_parameters()\n        constant(self.att, 1.)\n        self.lin_l.reset_parameters()\n        self.lin_r.reset_parameters()\n        self.lin_v.reset_parameters()\n\n    def get_x_r(self, x):\n        return self.lin_r(x)\n\n    def get_x_l(self, x):\n        return self.lin_l(x)\n\n    def get_x_v(self, x):\n        return self.lin_v(x)\n\n    def compute_score(self, x_i, x_j, index, ptr, size_i):\n        return torch.sum(F.leaky_relu(x_i + x_j, self.negative_slope) * self.att, dim=-1, keepdim=True)",
    "Experiment Result": "The core implementation is found in `lcat/pyg/generalgat.py`'s `GeneralGATLayer`, which is then specialized by `GATv1Layer` and `GATv2Layer` in `lcat/pyg/gatv1.py` and `lcat/pyg/gatv2.py` respectively.\n\n**For CAT (Graph Convolutional Attention Layer):**\n-   The `GeneralGATLayer` is instantiated with `mode='cat'`. This configures the layer to use `self.convolve = True`, `self.learn_l1 = False`, and `self.learn_l2 = False`.\n-   This results in fixed lambda parameters: `self.lmbda = 1.0` (λ1) and `self.lmbda2 = 1.0` (λ2).\n-   The `get_x_agg` method then computes the input features for attention (`˜hi`) using the formula `(hi + ∑ℓ∈Ni hℓ) / (1 + |Ni|)`, effectively averaging neighborhood features for attention score computation.\n\n**For L-CAT (Learnable Graph Convolutional Attention Network):**\n-   The `GeneralGATLayer` is instantiated with `mode='lcat'`. This configures the layer to use `self.convolve = True`, `self.learn_l1 = True`, and `self.learn_l2 = True`.\n-   This enables learnable lambda parameters: `self.lmbda` (λ1) and `self.lmbda2` (λ2), which are transformed via sigmoid functions: `torch.sigmoid(10 ** self.lmbda_ - 6)` and `torch.sigmoid(10 ** (2.2 - self.lmbda2_) - 6)`.\n-   The `get_x_agg` method interpolates the input features for attention (`˜hi`) using these learned λ1 and λ2 as follows: `λ1_learned * (hi + λ2_learned * ∑ℓ∈Ni hℓ) / (1 + λ2_learned * |Ni|)`. This allows dynamic interpolation between GCN, GAT, and CAT operations.\n\n**Experimental Settings (as derived from `tests/test_lcat_pyg.py`):**\n-   **Layer Type:** `GATv1Layer` or `GATv2Layer` (both inherit from `GeneralGATLayer` to provide attention computation details).\n-   **Dataset:** MUTAG from `torch_geometric.datasets.TUDataset`.\n-   **Batch Size:** 8.\n-   **Input Channels (`in_channels`):** Determined dynamically from `batch.x.shape[1]` of the dataset.\n-   **Output Channels (`out_channels`):** 32.\n-   **Heads:** 1, 2, or 4.\n-   **Negative Slope (for Leaky ReLU in attention score computation):** 0.2.\n-   **Add Self-Loops:** Configurable as True or False.\n-   **Bias:** True.\n-   **Aggregation for heads (after message passing):** 'mean'.\n-   **Share Weights for Score/Value:** `share_weights_score=False`, `share_weights_value=False`.\n-   **Lambda Parameter Initialization (for `mode='lcat'`):**\n    -   `lmbda_` (for λ1): Initialized to `math.log10(6)`. This leads to `sigmoid(0.0) = 0.5` for λ1.\n    -   `lmbda2_` (for λ2): Initialized to `2.2 - math.log10(6)`. This leads to `sigmoid(0.0) = 0.5` for λ2.\n    Thus, both learnable interpolation parameters start at a neutral mid-point."
}{
    "Title": "Sketch-GNN: Scalable Graph Neural Networks with Sublinear Training Complexity",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Sketch-GNN, a novel framework for training Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with sublinear time and memory complexity with respect to graph size. The key problem addressed is the scalability of GNNs on large graphs, where existing methods either incur prohibitive memory costs (full-batch) or exponentially growing computational complexities (mini-batch). Sketch-GNN achieves this by training GNNs on compact sketches of graph adjacency and node embeddings. Main contributions include a novel protocol using polynomial tensor-sketch (PTS) theory for sketching non-linear activations and graph convolution matrices (unlike previous work sketching linear weights/gradients), and a learnable locality-sensitive hashing (LSH) technique to improve sketch quality adaptively. Experiments demonstrate scalability and competitive performance against full-size GNNs and other scalable methods on large graph benchmarks.",
    "Methodology": "Sketch-GNN's methodology centers on sketching high-dimensional graph data structures (n x n adjacency matrix and n x d node feature matrix) to lower dimensions (c x c and c x d respectively). The core technique is Polynomial Tensor Sketch (PTS), which approximates non-linear activation functions by expanding them into a power series and then sketching each power using count/tensor sketches. The coefficients of this power series are learned online via gradient descent with L2 regularization. For approximating GNN update rules, Sketch-GNN propagates sketches of node representations between layers using pairwise-independent count sketches and FFT-based tensor sketch formulations. To enhance sketch quality and adapt to changing hidden representations, the framework employs a learnable Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH) technique. This involves updating LSH projection matrices using a sampled triplet loss on a dynamically selected subset of nodes (those with largest gradient signals), without needing to unsketch full representations. Furthermore, to avoid O(n) complexity in loss evaluation, the paper introduces sparse forward-pass and back-propagation, utilizing LSH hash tables per class to identify nodes with poor predictions and compute loss only on a subset.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The Sketch-GNN algorithm was evaluated on both small and large graph benchmarks for node classification tasks. Small graph benchmarks included Cora and Citeseer. Large graph benchmarks were ogbn-arxiv (169K nodes, 1.2M edges), Reddit (233K nodes, 11.6M edges), and ogbn-products (2.4M nodes, 61.9M edges) obtained from Open Graph Benchmark (OGB) and PyTorch Geometric. Comparisons were made against 'full-graph' (oracle) training, and other scalable methods: graph coarsening (Coarsening), dataset condensation (GCond), linearized GNNs (SGC), sampling-based (GraphSAINT), and historical-embedding based (VQ-GNN). GNN architectures tested included GCN, GraphSAGE, and GAT. Performance was primarily measured by test accuracy, while efficiency was assessed through preprocessing time, training time, and memory usage. Experiments were conducted on Nvidia RTX 2080Ti GPUs with Xeon CPUs. Training involved 3-layer GNNs with 128 hidden dimensions, a learning rate of 0.001, and the Adam optimizer. For Sketch-GNN, the polynomial order (number of sketches) 'r' was set to 3, with an L2 penalty on learnable coefficients (λ 0.01-0.1) and triplet loss hyperparameters (α=0.1, t+ > t- > 0). LSH hash functions were updated every 10 epochs after an initial 5 epochs.",
    "Limitations": "The major limitations of Sketch-GNN are that the sketched nonlinear activations are inherently less expressive than their original counterparts. This reduced expressiveness, combined with the accumulated error from sketching across layers, makes it challenging to apply the framework effectively to much deeper GNNs. Additionally, the paper notes that its theoretical assumptions and derived error bounds may not perfectly align with practical experimental observations. Another limitation mentioned is that the current implementation is not fully optimized with the most advanced libraries.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include addressing the challenges of less expressive sketched nonlinear activations and mitigating the accumulated error of sketching, particularly to enable the training of much deeper GNNs. Another promising area is to apply the proposed neural network sketching techniques to other types of data and neural networks beyond GNNs. Specifically, the paper also points to the challenging problem of bounding the error of sketches and coefficients learned directly by gradients, which is deferred for future studies. Furthermore, combining Sketch-GNN with subgraph sampling is suggested as a way to scale to extremely large graphs, potentially avoiding issues with decreasing sketch ratios under fixed memory constraints.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nimport torch\nfrom torch import nn\nfrom torch.autograd import Function, Variable\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\n\nimport pdb\n\n\nclass VectorQuantizer(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, num_embeddings, embedding_dim, commitment_cost=0.5, holistic_cost=0.1):\n        super        (VectorQuantizer, self).__init__()\n\n        self._embedding_dim = embedding_dim\n        self._num_embeddings = num_embeddings\n\n        self._embedding = nn.Embedding(self._num_embeddings, self._embedding_dim)\n        self._embedding.weight.data.uniform_(-1 / self._num_embeddings, 1 / self._num_embeddings)\n        self._commitment_cost = commitment_cost\n        self._holistic_cost = holistic_cost\n\n    def kmeans_init(self, kmeans_centroids):\n        self._embedding.weight = nn.Parameter(kmeans_centroids)\n\n    def get_codebook(self):\n        return self._embedding.weight.data\n\n    def forward(self, inputs):\n        input_shape = inputs.shape\n\n        # Flatten input\n        flat_input = inputs.view(-1, self._embedding_dim)\n\n        # Calculate distances\n        distances = (torch.sum(flat_input ** 2, dim=1, keepdim=True)\n                     + torch.sum(self._embedding.weight ** 2, dim=1)\n                     - 2 * torch.matmul(flat_input, self._embedding.weight.t()))\n\n        # Encoding\n        encoding_indices = torch.argmin(distances, dim=1).unsqueeze(1)\n        encodings = torch.zeros(encoding_indices.shape[0], self._num_embeddings, device=inputs.device)\n        encodings.scatter_(1, encoding_indices, 1)\n\n        # Quantize and unflatten\n        quantized = torch.matmul(encodings, self._embedding.weight).view(input_shape)\n\n        # Loss\n        e_latent_loss = F.mse_loss(quantized.detach(), inputs)\n        q_latent_loss = F.mse_loss(quantized, inputs.detach())\n        loss = self._holistic_cost * (q_latent_loss + self._commitment_cost * e_latent_loss)\n\n        quantized = inputs + (quantized - inputs).detach()\n        # avg_probs = torch.mean(encodings, dim=0)\n        # perplexity = torch.exp(-torch.sum(avg_probs * torch.log(avg_probs + 1e-10)))\n\n        return loss, quantized, encodings, encoding_indices\n        # return loss, quantized.contiguous(), perplexity, encodings, encoding_indices\n\n\nclass VectorQuantizerEMA(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, num_embeddings, embedding_dim, commitment_cost=0.5, decay=0.99, epsilon=1e-24,\n add_flag  else 0\n\n        self._embedding_dim = embedding_dim\n        self._num_embeddings = num_embeddings\n        self._commitment_cost = commitment_cost\n        self._warm_up_flag =       # Laplace smoothing of the cluster size\n            if self._warm_up_flag :\n                n = torch.sum(self._ema_cluster_size.data)\n                self._ema_cluster_size.data = (\n      mean = torch.mean(inputs, dim=0, keepdim=True).detach()\n        std = torch.sqrt(torch.var(inputs, dim=0, keepdim=True) + self._epsilon).detach()\n        self.mean = mean\n        self.std = not None\n        assert alpha_l is not None\n\n        # Trick 1\n        scale = torch.sqrt(torch.max(alpha_l)**2+1) * torch.sqrt(torch.max(alpha_r)**2+1)\n        alpha_l = alpha_l / scale\n        alpha_r = alpha_r / scale\n\n        # pdb.set_trace()\n\n\n        if self.add_self_loops:\n            if isinstance(edge_index, Tensor):\n                num_nodes = x_l.size(0)\n                if layout='coo')\n        else:\n            return out\n\n    def message(self, x_j: Tensor, alpha_j: Tensor, alpha_i: OptTensor,\n                index: Tensor, ptr: OptTensor, size_i: Optional[int],\n                edge_weight: OptTensor) -> Tensor:\n\n\n        alpha = alpha_j if alpha_i is None else alpha_j + alpha_i\n        alpha = F.leaky_relu(alpha, self.negative_slope)\n        alpha = vq_softmax(alpha, index, ptr, size_i)\n\n        self._alpha = alpha\n        alpha = F.dropout(alpha, p=self.dropout, training=self.training)\n\n        # Trick 2\n        alpha = alpha * edge_weight.unsqueeze(-1)\n\n        return x_j * alpha.unsqueeze(-1)\n\n\nclass Transformer(torch.nn.Module) :\n    def __init__(self, num_D):\n        super(Transformer, self).__init__()\n        self.num_D = num_D\n\n    def forward(self, X_B, X_bar):\n\n        C_BM = torch.mm(X_B, X_bar.t()) / math.sqrt(self.num_D)\n        # c_max = torch.max(C_BM)\n\n        c_max = torch.max(torch.sum(torch.cat([X_B, X_bar], dim=0)**2, dim=1))\n        C_BM = C_BM / c_max\n        C_BM = torch.exp(C_BM)\n\n        # trick2\n        X_B_output = torch.mm(C_BM/torch.sum(C_BM, dim=1, keepdim=True), X_bar)\n        X_bar_output = torch.mm(C_BM.t()/torch.sum(C_BM.t(), dim=1, keepdim=True), X_B)\n\n        return torch.cat([X_B_output, X_bar_output], dim=0)\n\nimport     "Title": "HyperAttention: Long-context Attention in Near-Linear Time",
    "Main Contributions": "HyperAttention addresses the quadratic computational challenges of long-context attention in Large Language Models (LLMs) by introducing an approximate attention mechanism. It achieves near-linear time complexity (O(d * n^(1+o(1)) + d * nnz(MH))) without requiring bounded entries or bounded stable rank, provided two fine-grained parameters (max column L2-norm and ratio of row norms after removing large entries) are small. The method offers practical efficiency and supports causal masking, which was a limitation in prior works. Empirically, HyperAttention significantly speeds up attention computations (e.g., 50x acceleration for forward/backward propagation at n=131k without causal masking, 5x with causal masking) while maintaining performance on pre-trained LLMs on long-context benchmarks like LongBench, with minimal perplexity increase.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves efficiently approximating the diagonal scaling matrix D and the matrix product of the softmax matrix D^-1A and value matrix V. For estimating D, the method first identifies dominant entries in the attention matrix using a Hamming sorted Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH) algorithm called sortLSH, which generates a sparse mask (Algorithm 1). This is combined with random sampling of a small subset of keys to estimate row sums of unmasked entries (Algorithm 2). The approximation of the matrix product D^-1AV is achieved through subsampling, where a sampling matrix S is constructed based on squared row norms of V (or uniform sampling in practice), leveraging Approximate Matrix Multiplication (AMM) techniques (Lemma 2). For causal masking, a recursive method (Algorithm 4) is employed to approximate the diagonal DC by partitioning the masked attention matrix into smaller causal and unmasked blocks, applying the unmasked approximation algorithm to the latter. The approach introduces fine-grained parameters to analyze time complexity, allowing for linear time even when matrix entries are unbounded or stable rank is large.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on a single A100 GPU with 40 GB memory, using FlashAttention 2 as the baseline for exact attention computation. HyperAttention was evaluated by 'monkey patching' (replacing) attention layers in pre-trained LLMs: chatglm2-6b-32k and phi-1.5. Performance was measured by perplexity on LongBench, a collection of long context benchmark datasets including single/multiple-document question answering, summarization, few-shot learning, synthetic tasks, and code completion, with context lengths trimmed to 32,768 tokens. Speedup was measured in wall-clock times for forward and forward+backward operations across varying sequence lengths (4,096 to 131,072) for a single self-attention layer, with and without causal masking. HyperAttention parameters included a bucket size 'b' and number of sampled columns 'm' set to 256. The assumption regarding the squared L2-norm of columns in D^-1A was empirically verified using the T2T-ViT model on ImageNet and chatglm2-6b-32k on the LongBench narrative-qa dataset, varying sequence lengths from 1k to 9k.",
    "Limitations": "The practical algorithm for causal masking, while theoretically having the same complexity, incurs additional runtime due to operations like partitioning Q, K, V, and merging attention outputs. Replacing attention layers with HyperAttention generally leads to some performance degradation in LLMs, though its impact varies by task, with summarization and code completion showing more robustness. The theoretical guarantees rely on assumptions that specific fine-grained parameters (max column L2-norm and ratio of row norms after removing large entries) remain small, and that the column norms of the softmax matrix exhibit a relatively uniform distribution (empirically verified). Additionally, the method assumes the large entries mask (MH) is sparse and that procedures for finding large entries (e.g., LSH, CountSketch) are fast.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that the significant speedups offered by HyperAttention 'open the door to scale self-attention not only for inference but also for training or fine-tuning the LLMs to fit in significantly long sequences,' indicating a key future research direction in applying and optimizing HyperAttention for training pipelines. Further work could also focus on optimizing the practical runtime for causal masking, given its current increased operational overhead. Additionally, understanding why certain tasks like summarization and code completion are more robust to approximate attention layers could lead to task-specific insights and optimizations.",
    "Experiment Code": "File Path: models/attention/angular_lsh.py\nContent:\n\nimport torch\n\n\nclass AngularLSH(torch.nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, num_projs, dim, rng=None):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.num_projs = num_projs\n\n        if num_projs > 0:\n            self.register_buffer('proj_dir', torch.randn(dim + (num_projs,), generator=rng), persistent=False)\n            self.register_buffer('perm', self._unit_hamming_distance_array(self.num_projs), persistent=False)\n            self.register_buffer('enc_vec', 2 ** torch.arange(self.num_projs).view(1, 1, 1, -1), persistent=False)\n            \n    def _unit_hamming_distance_array(self, size_n):\n        if size_n == 1:\n            return torch.tensor([0, 1])\n        a = self._unit_hamming_distance_array(size_n - 1)\n        return torch.concat([a, torch.flip(a, dims=[0]) + 2 ** (size_n - 1)], 0)\n\n    def hash(self, mat):\n        if self.num_projs < 0:\n            return torch.zeros(mat.shape[:-1], device=mat.device, dtype=torch.int32)\n        mask = torch.einsum('...nd,...dr -> ...nr', mat, self.proj_dir)\n        mask = mask > 0\n        bin_ids = (mask * self.enc_vec).sum(-1)\n        return self.perm[bin_ids]\n    \n    def __repr__(self):\n        return f\"AngularLSH(num_proj={self.num_projs}, proj_dir.shape={self.proj_dir.shape})\"\n\nFile Path: models/attention/flash_attn_triton_for_hyper.py\nContent:\n\"\"\"\n*Experimental* implementation of FlashAttention in Triton.\nTested with triton==2.0.0.dev20221202.\nTriton 2.0 has a new backend (MLIR) but seems like it doesn't yet work for head dimensions\nother than 64:\nhttps://github.com/openai/triton/blob/d376020f90002757eea3ea9475d4f7cfc2ec5ead/python/triton/ops/flash_attention.py#L207\nWe'll update this implementation with the new Triton backend once this is fixed.\n\nWe use the FlashAttention implementation from Phil Tillet a starting point.\nhttps://github.com/openai/triton/blob/master/python/tutorials/06-fused-attention.py\n\nChanges:\n- Implement both causal and non-causal attention.\n- Implement both self-attention and cross-attention.\n- Support arbitrary seqlens (not just multiples of 128), for both forward and backward.\n- Support all head dimensions up to 128 (not just 16, 32, 64, 128), for both forward and backward.\n- Support attention bias.\n- Speed up the forward pass a bit, and only store the LSE instead of m and l.\n- Make the backward for d=128 much faster by reducing register spilling.\n- Optionally parallelize the backward pass across seqlen_k, to deal with the case of\nsmall batch size * nheads.\n\nCaution:\n- This is an *experimental* implementation. The forward pass should be quite robust but\nI'm not 100% sure that the backward pass doesn't have race conditions (due to the Triton compiler).\n- This implementation has only been tested on A100.\n- If you plan to use headdim other than 64 and 128, you should test for race conditions\n(due to the Triton compiler), as done in tests/test_flash_attn.py\n\"test_flash_attn_triton_race_condition\". I've tested and fixed many race conditions\nfor different head dimensions (40, 48, 64, 128, 80, 88, 96), but I'm still not 100% confident\nthat there are none left for other head dimensions.\n\nDifferences between this Triton version and the CUDA version:\n- Triton version doesn't support dropout.\n- Triton forward is generally faster than CUDA forward, while Triton backward is\ngenerally slower than CUDA backward. Overall Triton forward + backward is slightly slower\nthan CUDA forward + backward.\n- Triton version doesn't support different sequence lengths in a batch (i.e., RaggedTensor/NestedTensor).\n- Triton version supports attention bias, while CUDA version doesn't.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport math\n\nimport torch\nimport triton\nimport triton.language as tl\n\n\n# Disabling autotune for now, set num_warps=4 if headdim=64 and num_warps=8 if headdim=128\n# @triton.autotune(\n#     configs=[\n#         triton.Config({\"BLOCK_M\": 128, \"BLOCK_N\": 128}, num_warps=4, num_stages=1),\n#         # -- update statistics\n        m_i = m_ij\n        l_i_new = tl.exp(lse_i - m_ij) + l_ij\n        lse_i = m_ij + tl.log(l_i_new)\n\n    o_scale = tl.exp(m_i - lse_i)\n    # BUG: have to store and immediately load\n    tl.store(t_ptrs, o_scale)\n    o_scale = tl.load(t_ptrs)\n    acc_o = acc_o * o_scale[:, None]\n    # Make sure that the last dimension is contiguous\n        q, k, v = [x if x.stride(-1) == 1 else x.contiguous() for x in [q, k, v]]\n        o, lse, ctx.softmax_scale = _flash_attn_forward(\n            q, k, v, bias=bias, causal=causal, softmax_scale=softmax_scale\n        )\n        ctx.save_for_backward(q, k, v, o, lse, bias)\n        ctx.causal = causal\n        return o, lse\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def backward(ctx, do, dlse_use_needed=None):\n        q, k, v, o, lse, bias = ctx.saved_tensors\n        # assert not ctx.needs_input_grad[3], \"FlashAttention does not support bias gradient yet\"\n        # Triton's autotune causes the Tensor._version to change, and so Pytorch autograd\n        # does a memcpy. Without causal masking\n        if not causal: \n            attn, lse = self.forward_no_causal_mask(query, key, value, scale)\n\n        # With causal masking\n        else:\n            if n_key <= self.min_seq_len:\n                if self.cuda:\n                    attn, lse = exact_attention_cuda(query, key, value, scale, causal=True)\n                else:\n                    attn, lse = exact_attention(query, key, value, scale, causal=True)\n            else:\n            \n                # If n_query is odd we pad inputs by adding all-zero rows\n                if n_query % 2:\n                    query = torch.nn.functional.pad(query, (0,0,0,1), mode='constant',value=0.)\n                    key = torch.nn.functional.pad(key, (0,0,0,1), mode='constant',value=0.)\n                    value = torch.nn.functional.pad(value, (0,0,0,1), mode='constant',value=0.)\n\n                q_bd = query.view(batch_size, 2*n_heads, query.shape[2]//2, query.shape[-1])\n                k_bd = key.view(batch_size, 2*n_heads, key.shape[2]//2, key.shape[-1])\n                v_bd = value.view(batch_size, 2*n_heads, key.shape[2]//2, value.shape[-1])\n        \n                attn_bd, lse_bd = self.forward(q_bd, k_bd, v_bd, scale, True, True)\n                \n                if attn_bd.shape[2] not in attn_bd.stride():\n                    attn_bd = attn_bd.contiguous()\n                attn_bd = attn_bd.view(batch_size, n_heads, -1, dim)\n\n                if lse_bd.shape[2] not in lse_bd.stride():\n                    lse_bd = lse_bd.contiguous()\n                lse_bd = lse_bd.view(batch_size, n_heads, -1, 1)\n\n                attn_unmasked, lse_unmasked = self.forward_no_causal_mask(\n                    query[:, :, key.shape[2]//2:, :],\n                    key[:, :, :key.shape[2]//2, :], \n                    value[:, :, :key.shape[2]//2, :], scale)\n\n                attn_up, lse_up = attn_bd[:,:,:query.shape[2]//2,:], lse_bd[:,:,:query.shape[2]//2,:]\n                attn_down, lse_down = add_self_attentions(\n                    attn_bd[:,:,query.shape[2]//2:,:],\n                    lse_bd[:,:,query.shape[2]//2:,:],\n                    attn_unmasked,\n                    lse_unmasked)\n\n                attn = torch.cat((attn_up, attn_down), dim=-2)\n                lse = torch.cat((lse_up, lse_down), dim=-2)\n\n                # If n_query was odd exclude the last rows\n                if n_query % 2:\n                    attn = attn[:,:,:-1,:]\n                    lse = lse[:,:,:-1,:]\n\n        if not return_lse:\n            return attn\n        else:\n            return attn, lse\n\n\n    def forward_no_causal_mask(self, query, key, value, scale):\n\n        batch_size, head_size, n_query, dim = query.shape\n        n_key = key.shape[2]\n\n        if self.min_seq_len > n_query:\n            if self.cuda:\n                return exact_attention_cuda(query, key, value, scale, causal=False)\n            else:\n                return exact_attention(query, key, value, scale, causal=False)\n\n        # 1. Sorted block-diagonal via sortLSH\n        _, query_sort_idx = torch.sort(self.lsh.hash(query), dim=2, stable=True) # batch_size x head_size x n\n        _, key_sort_idx = torch.sort(self.lsh.hash(key), dim=2, stable=True)\n        query_sort_idx_inv = torch.argsort(query_sort_idx, dim=2, stable=True) # for recovering the row order\n\n        key_block_size = self.block_size\n\n        query_sorted = indexing(query, query_sort_idx, key_block_size)\n        key_sorted = indexing(key, key_sort_idx, key_block_size)\n        value_sorted = indexing(value, key_sort_idx, key_block_size)\n\n        if key_block_size > 0:\n\n            num_blocks = key_sorted.shape[2] // key_block_size\n            query_block_size = query_sorted.shape[2] // num_blocks\n\n            # Reshape tensors to [batch_size*head_size, 1, block_size, dim] as Flash-attn only allows 4d-tensors\n            query_split_per_block = query_sorted.view(-1, 1, query_block_size, dim)\n            key_split_per_block = key_sorted.view(-1, 1, key_block_size, dim)\n            value_split_per_block = value_sorted.view(-1, 1, key_block_size, dim)\n\n            if self.cuda:\n                attn_block, lse_block = exact_attention_cuda(\n                    query_split_per_block, key_split_per_block, value_split_per_block,\n                    softmax_scale=scale, causal=False)\n            else:\n                attn_block, lse_block = exact_attention(\n                    query_split_per_block, key_split_per_block, value_split_per_block,\n                    softmax_scale=scale, causal=False)\n\n            if attn_block.shape[2] not in attn_block.stride():\n                attn_block = attn_block.contiguous()\n            attn_block = attn_block.view(batch_size, head_size, query_sorted.shape[2], -1)\n\n            if lse_block.shape[2] not in lse_block.stride():\n                lse_block = lse_block.contiguous()\n            lse_block = lse_block.view(batch_size, head_size, query_sorted.shape[2], -1)\n\n            # 2. Residual low-rank part via uniform sampling\n        # Sample indices uniformly at random\n        sample_size = self.sample_size\n        if sample_size > 0 and (n_query > query_block_size) and (n_key > key_block_size):\n            sampled_set = torch.randint(n_key, size=(batch_size, head_size, sample_size), device=query_sorted.device)\n            \n            # Compute mask for hiding A_ij computed in block-diagonal attention\n            offset_n = rearrange(torch.arange(n_query, device=query_sorted.device), 'n -> 1 n 1')\n            weights = n_key / sample_size\n            value_subset = indexing(value_sorted, sampled_set)\n            key_subset = indexing(key_sorted, sampled_set)\n\n            if not self.cuda:\n                block_mask = (offset_n // query_block_size) == (sampled_set // key_block_size).view(-1, 1, sample_size)\n                block_mask = block_mask.view(batch_size, head_size, -1, sample_size)\n                block_mask = block_mask.to(query_sorted.dtype)\n                block_mask *= torch.finfo(query_sorted.dtype).min # adding -inf added to QK^T\n\n                attn_res, lse_res = exact_attention(query_sorted, key_subset, value_subset, scale, causal=False, bias=block_mask)\n            else:\n                attn_res, lse_res = exact_attention_cuda(query_sorted, key_subset, value_subset, scale, causal=False)\n            lse_res = lse_res + math.log(weights)\n\n            # Add two attentions\n            if key_block_size > 0:\n                attn, lse = add_self_attentions(attn_block, lse_block, attn_res, lse_res)\n            else:\n                attn, lse = attn_res, lse_res\n        else:\n            attn, lse = attn_block, lse_block\n\n        # Re-order rows with the     "Title": "RSC: Accelerate Graph Neural Networks Training via Randomized Sparse Computations",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of accelerating Graph Neural Network (GNN) training, which is time-consuming due to sparse graph-based operations (SpMM) that are difficult for current hardware to optimize. Unlike dense matrix operations, sparse operations pose unique challenges for approximation due to irregular data formats and inefficient sampling. The main contribution is a novel approximation framework called Randomized Sparse Computation (RSC), which controls the accuracy-efficiency trade-off by optimizing computation resource allocation layer-wisely and epoch-wisely. RSC can achieve up to 11.6x speedup for a single sparse operation and 1.6x end-to-end wall-clock time speedup with negligible (approx 0.3%) accuracy drop.",
    "Methodology": "RSC employs `top-k sampling` to approximate sparse matrix-dense matrix multiplication (SpMM) operations. Based on theoretical and empirical analysis, approximation is only applied to SpMM operations in the backward pass to maintain unbiased gradients and minimize accuracy loss, while the forward pass remains exact. To manage the accuracy-efficiency trade-off, RSC proposes a `layer-wise resource allocation` strategy formulated as a constrained optimization problem. This problem minimizes the relative approximation error across layers under a total FLOPs budget, solved efficiently using a `greedy algorithm`. Additionally, RSC incorporates two key mechanisms: a `caching mechanism` that reuses previously sampled sparse matrices across nearby iterations (e.g., every 10 steps) to reduce the overhead of slicing sparse matrices, and a `switching mechanism` that uses approximated operations during most of the training but reverts to original, exact sparse operations in the final stages (e.g., last 20% of epochs) to improve convergence and minimize accuracy drop.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The effectiveness of RSC is evaluated using four large-scale graph benchmarks: Reddit, Yelp, ogbn-proteins, and ogbn-products. Experiments are conducted under both mini-batch training (integrating RSC with GraphSAINT) and full-batch training (integrating with GCN, GraphSAGE with MEAN aggregator, and GCNII). All experiments are performed on a server equipped with four NVIDIA 3090 GPUs, four AMD EPYC 7282 CPUs, and 252GB host memory, using PyTorch and PyTorch Geometric. Key hyperparameters include the overall FLOPs budget C (varied at 0.1, 0.3, 0.5), a greedy algorithm step size alpha (0.02|V|), sampling/caching frequency (every 10 steps), and switching point (RSC for 80% epochs, then exact operations for 20%). Performance is measured by wall-clock time speedup (Tbaseline/Trsc) on GPUs and test accuracy/F1-micro/AUC, with results averaged over ten random trials.",
    "Limitations": "First, the speedup of RSC is inherently limited because it only replaces sparse operations in the backward pass to guarantee model accuracy. Second, the current framework does not directly cover Graph Neural Networks that rely on scatter-and-gather operations (e.g., GAT) instead of SpMM. While the sampling, caching, and switching mechanisms could be applicable, the resource allocation algorithm (Algorithm 1) would require tailored error bounds and computation cost modeling specific to scatter-and-gather operations.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work involves exploring RSC for GNNs that utilize scatter-and-gather operations. This would specifically entail developing tailored error bounds and computation cost modeling for these operations, to be integrated into the resource allocation algorithm (Equation 4) of RSC.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null
