    "Title": "Bandit Samplers for Training Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "Addresses the intractability of computing optimal sampling distributions for training Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), especially attentive GNNs, where node embeddings and learned weights dynamically change. Proposes a novel approach that formulates the optimization of sampling variance as an adversary bandit problem. Introduces two bandit sampling algorithms, GNN-BS (Multi-Armed Bandit) and GNN-BS.M (Multi-Armed Bandit with Multiple Plays), which are applicable to both GCNs and more general attentive GNN architectures like GAT and GeniePath. Provides theoretical guarantees that the proposed algorithms asymptotically approach the optimal sampling variance within a factor of 3. Empirically demonstrates superior performance in convergence speed, accuracy (Micro F1 scores), and reduced sampling variance compared to state-of-the-art GCN and attentive GNN training methods across multiple benchmark datasets.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves reformulating the problem of optimizing GNN sampling variance as an adversary bandit problem, where rewards are dynamically related to node embeddings and learned weights. Two main algorithms are proposed: 1. GNN-BS, which operates under the Multi-Armed Bandit (MAB) setting, samples a single neighbor `k` times, and updates the sampling distribution `q_t_i` using the EXP3 algorithm. The reward for selecting a neighbor is defined as the negative derivative of the effective variance. 2. GNN-BS.M, designed for the MAB with multiple plays setting, utilizes the DepRound sampler to select a `k`-element subset of neighbors once. It approximates the effective variance using Jensen's inequality, and updates `q_t_i` using EXP3.M. For attentive GNNs, which involve unnormalized attention values, the method introduces 'adjusted feedback attention values' to approximate the true attention coefficients. The overall framework involves iterative sampling, unbiased estimation, GNN model forward/backward passes, and reward-based updates to the sampling distribution.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The proposed methods were evaluated on GCN, Graph Attention Networks (GAT), and GeniePath architectures, all configured with 2 layers. Experiments were conducted using five public benchmark datasets: Cora, Pubmed, PPI, Reddit, and Flickr, along with an additional evaluation on the OGB protein dataset. Performance was measured primarily by Micro F1 scores for node classification tasks and an analysis of sample variances. Comparison methods included existing 'layer sampling' approaches (GraphSAGE, FastGCN, LADIES, AS-GCN), a variance-reduced estimator (S-GCN), and 'graph sampling' techniques (ClusterGCN, GraphSAINT), as well as their attentive GNN variants (AS-GAT, GraphSAINT-GAT). Hyperparameters such as learning rate, L2-norm regularizers, and dropout rates were tuned via grid search. Specific sample sizes (`k`) were set per dataset (e.g., 1 for Cora/Pubmed, 10 for PPI/Reddit), and batch sizes were uniform (256) for layer sampling methods. Models were selected based on the best validation performance, and final test results were reported as the mean and standard deviation over multiple runs (3 for benchmarks, 10 for OGB).",
    "Limitations": "The optimal sampling distribution for general GNNs is computationally intractable because it requires full, a priori knowledge of changing neighbors' hidden embeddings or learned weights. Existing importance sampling methods are suboptimal for GCNs and not applicable to general GNNs as they assume fixed weights or ignore embedding norms. The theoretical derivation of the bandit samplers currently follows node-wise sampling, with extension to layer-wise sampling left as future work. The GNN-BS algorithm, which repeats 1-arm selection `k` times, is acknowledged to not strictly follow the rigorous MAB setting (though GNN-BS.M addresses this). For GNN-BS.M, the effective variance is based on an approximation using Jensen's inequality. Additionally, 'graph sampling' approaches, while fast under specific conditions, are limited by sensitivity to graph partitioning and the assumption that all vertices have labels, making them less flexible than layer sampling in many practical scenarios.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could focus on extending the bandit sampler derivation to incorporate layer-wise sampling approaches, which was noted as a limitation in the current work. Further exploration into other bandit problem formulations beyond the adversary bandit setting used in this paper is also suggested. While the method already applies to attentive GNNs, exploring its applicability and optimization for an even wider range of complex and evolving GNN architectures remains a promising avenue. Although not explicitly stated, improvements in memory efficiency for very large graphs and complex GNNs could also be a relevant direction, considering memory issues encountered with some baselines on large datasets.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nimport tensorflow as tf\n\nflags = tf.app.flags\nFLAGS = flags.FLAGS\n\ndef attention_mechanism(name, v, W_s, W_d, V, cur_embed, left, right, n2n):\n    # a_{i,j} \\propto v^\\top tanh (W_s \\mu_i + W_d \\mu_j)\n    if name == 'generalized_linear':\n        tl = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=left, b=cur_embed) # m by k\n        tl = tf.matmul(tl, W_s)\n        tr = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=right, b=cur_embed)\n        tr = tf.matmul(tr, W_d)\n        t = tf.nn.tanh(tf.add(tl,tr))\n        t = tf.matmul(t, tf.reshape(v, [-1,1]))\n        sparse_attention = tf.SparseTensor(n2n.indices, tf.reshape(t, [-1]), n2n.dense_shape)\n        sparse_attention = tf.sparse_softmax(sparse_attention)\n    else:\n        sys.exit(-1)\n    return sparse_attention\n\n\nclass GeniePath(Model):\n    def _build(self):\n        # ... The `sparse_attention_l0` (unnormalized attention values from the first GNN layer) is then used to `sampler.update` the sampling probabilities.\n- **Evaluation**: Uses `ogb.nodeproppred.Evaluator`.\n    - For `ogbn-proteins`: `rocauc` (ROC AUC score).\n    - For `ogbn-products`: `acc` (accuracy).\n- **Runs**: The entire training and evaluation process is repeated `10` times (from `rnd = 1` to `10`).\n- **Model Saving**: Best validation performance per round leads to model saving. The initial model (before any training) is also saved and restored for each round.\n- **Final Result**: Mean and standard deviation of test scores across 10 runs are reported."

    "Title": "KDEformer: Accelerating Transformers via Kernel Density Estimation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces KDEformer, a novel algorithm to accelerate Transformer attention mechanisms by addressing the quadratic time and memory complexities of dot-product attention. The key insight is reducing the computation of partition functions in the softmax denominator to a variant of the Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) problem. An efficient KDE solver is then utilized to accelerate the multiplication of the softmax matrix with the values matrix via subsampling-based fast matrix products. KDEformer approximates attention in sub-quadratic time with provable spectral norm bounds, a significant advancement over prior methods that only provided entry-wise error bounds. Empirically, KDEformer outperforms other attention approximations in accuracy, memory, and runtime, achieving better generative scores on BigGAN image generation with over 4x speedup and over 18x speedup on ImageNet classification with T2T-ViT with less than 0.5% accuracy drop.",
    "Methodology": "KDEformer's methodology involves reducing the attention mechanism's core computations to a generalized Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) problem. Specifically, the diagonal scaling matrix D is estimated by viewing its entries as exponential kernel densities. The sampling matrix Π, crucial for approximating the attention output, is generated using sampling probabilities proportional to the column norms of the softmax matrix D^-1A, which are also approximated via weighted exponential kernel densities. The paper shows how to translate this weighted exponential KDE problem into a Gaussian KDE problem, for which efficient solvers exist (e.g., Charikar et al. [7]). The algorithm adaptively learns the density lower bound (eµ) required by the Gaussian KDE. For practical improvements, KDEformer employs Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH) to identify and subtract \"heavy\" elements of D^-1A, thereby reducing its stable rank and the required number of samples for approximate matrix multiplication (AMM). A GPU-friendly angular LSH with equal-sized buckets and minimal spillover is also introduced.",
    "Experimental Setup": "KDEformer's performance was evaluated across several tasks and datasets: 1. Single Self-attention Layer Approximation: Using GloVe word embeddings (n=8,192, d=100) to measure relative spectral error, peak memory usage, FLOP count, and CPU-clock time against Reformer, Performer, and ScatterBrain. 2. Image Generation: Using a pre-trained BigGAN model on ImageNet (512x512 resolution) with Q, K, V dimensions (4,096x64, 1,024x64, 1,024x256) for generating 5,000 fake images, evaluated by Frechet Inception Distance (FID) and Inception Scores (IS). 3. ImageNet Classification: Using a pre-trained T2T-ViT model (24 layers) for image classification, applying KDEformer to 2 attention layers in the T2T module. Evaluated by top-1 accuracy on ImageNet validation dataset and FLOPS. 4. End-to-end Training: On the Long Range Arena (LRA) benchmark, including ListOps, Text, Image, Retrieval, and Pathfinder datasets (max sequence lengths up to 4,096), using a 2-layer transformer model. Evaluated by test accuracy, peak memory, and wall-clock time per batch.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical runtime bound for KDEformer, O(m + ε^-2d * n^(1+τ+o(1))), is strongly sub-quadratic for datasets of queries Q and keys K with a diameter `maxi,j∈[n] ||ki - qj||_2^2 = o(√d * log n)`. This specifically applies when the dimension 'd' is not too large, such as `d = o(log^2 n)`, for example, `d ≈ log^2 n / log log n`. The method also requires adaptively learning a density lower bound (eµ) in the Gaussian KDE procedure.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper suggests that the proposed KDEformer, being based on recent advances in KDE solvers, has a significant impact on other practical problems beyond Transformer acceleration. Any future progress on Gaussian density evaluation will immediately improve KDEformer's runtime. The authors also believe this method can be extended and applied to a wider range of practical problems involving long-sequence attention.",

{'open_problems': 'Memory/computation of GAT training is dominated by dense attention maps that are mostly useless. GATSY already masks the largest α·deg edges, but:\n1. Selecting top-k with exact sort costs O(deg log k) per node and still stores all logits before pruning.\n2. Degree-proportional k wastes budget on high–entropy nodes and over-prunes hub nodes with low entropy.\n3. 2:4 AMP sparsity helps only on Ampere+ GPUs; for others we still pay dense GEMMs.\n4. Quantisation noise is treated uniformly; low-entropy edges can bear more noise than high-entropy ones.\n5. Existing variance proofs ignore estimator bias introduced by approximate top-k search.\n6. No mechanism to re-activate edges pruned too early during training.\n7. Autotuning of all hyper-parameters (α, target‐variance ε, bit-budget) is manual.', 'methods': 'We propose GATSY-X: eXtremely compressed yet unbiased GAT training.\nA. HashTop-k with Error-Feedback\n   A1  Replace exact top-k by sortLSH (HyperAttention). Two-pass: (i) Hamming-LSH buckets give candidate set ≤2k. (ii) lightweight heap keeps k maxima. Reduces temporary memory to O(k).\n   A2  Store a sparse residual tensor R_t (same pattern as dropped edges) and add it back to logits next iteration – error-feedback guarantees unbiased expected logits.\nB. Information-Guided k\n   B1  Estimate per-node attention entropy H_i from previous mini-batch. Set k_i = ceil(β·deg·H_i/ log deg +1). β learns with EWMA to keep global edge budget.\n   B2  Proven variance bound tighter than degree rule; avoids over-pruning hubs.\nC. Noise-Aware Quantisation\n   C1  For kept edges, target bit-width b_e = max(2, ceil( b_max – γ·log σ̂_e )), where σ̂_e is running variance of message gradient. Compute with Welford streaming, no extra pass.\nD. Block-Sparse GEMM fallback\n   D1  When GPU tensor cores lack 2:4 support (Volta, RTX2xxx), regroup edges into 32×32 blocks (Ellpack-like) and invoke CUTLASS block-sparse kernels. Chooses 2:4 if available, else block32.\nE. Mask Re-activation\n   E1  Every T epochs, sample 5% of previously dropped edges, feed through network, and if their magnitude ranked in top-k′ they migrate into active set. Keeps training plastic.\nF. Tight Unbiasedness Proof\n   F1  Show that combining approximate top-k + error-feedback forms a martingale difference; gradient remains unbiased. Provide analytic extra variance term ≤ (1/k)·Var(R).\nG. Auto-Tune Controller\n   G1  Hyper-parameters (β, γ, global bit-budget B) optimised online by black-box optimizer (Hyperband) driven by memory profiler and validation accuracy proxy using 1-epoch look-ahead.\n', 'experimental_setup': 'Datasets: OGBN-Arxiv, Reddit, OGBN-Products, OGBN-Papers100M (to test scalability).\nModels: 2-layer GAT, 4-layer GATv2. Exact top-k vs HashTop-k\n  2. No error-feedback\n  3. Degree-based k vs info-guided k\n  4. Fixed 4-bit vs noise-aware bits\n  5. Re-activation off\n  6. Block32 vs dense GEMM on Turing', 'experimental_code': 'PyTorch ≥2.1.\nPseudo-snippet:\nclass HashTopK(Function):\n    @staticmethod\n    def forward(ctx, scores, k):\n        idx_cand = sortlsh(scores, 2*k)           # CUDA kernel\n        topk_idx = torch.topk(scores[idx_cand], k).indices\n        mask = torch.zeros_like(scores, dtype=torch.bool)\n        mask[idx_cand[topk_idx]] = True\n        ctx.save_for_backward(mask)\n        return mask\n    @staticmethod\n    def backward(ctx, g_mask):   # error-feedback route\n        mask, = ctx.saved_tensors\n        residual = (~mask).float() * g_mask\n        return residual, None\n\n# training loop adds residual buffer R to next logits\nscores = (q @ k.T)/sqrt(dh) + R_prev\nmask = HashTopK.apply(scores, k_i)\nR_next = (1-mask).float()*scores.detach()\n\nbit_e = bit_policy(var_estimate, b_max)\nquant_v = quantize(v, bit_e)\n', 'expected_result': '• Peak memory ↓ 8-10× vs FP32, 3-4× vs vanilla GATSY.\n• Speedup: A100 2:4 path 3-4×, RTX2080Ti 2.5× via block32 vs dense.\n• Accuracy gap ≤0.2% on all benchmarks; on Papers100M within 0.5% while using <16 GB.\n• Variance bound tight: measured / bound ratio <1.2.\n• Auto-tuner finds configs within 5% of best hand-tuned in <30 min.\n• Re-activation recovers 0.4% accuracy that would be lost after aggressive early pruning.', 'expected_conclusion': 'GATSY-X shows that approximate, information-theoretic masking plus adaptive quantisation delivers state-of-the-art training efficiency on attention-based GNNs across consumer- and data-centre-grade GPUs. Its error-feedback HashTop-k keeps gradients unbiased while halving temporary memory, and entropy-aware edge budgets outperform naive degree rules. The block-sparse fallback generalises beyond 2:4 hardware, making the method broadly deployable. GATSY-X therefore narrows the training cost gap between GATs and simpler GCNs, enabling long-context graph problems on single-GPU rigs.'}
